Banki flicked the leather wrapping aside, exposing the cold, polished edge of a trade bar. The platinum caught the furnace flame in a sharp, icy glint. "Deliver the iron. We have beasts to cage, and I will not lose stock to cheap latches."

## The Eldritch Hour

Brine, bone-ash grout, and the heavy, ozone stench of captive violence rendered the subterranean air of Sablehook almost unbreathable.

The descent into the lower containment levels always felt like dropping into the throat of a decaying leviathan. The temperature plummeted with every flagstone, the damp cold soaking through leather and linen to bite at the joints. The walls—hewn from massive, seamless blocks of spell-fused granite—swept continuous, dirty tracks of condensation down into the drainage channels.

The primary viewing corridor stretched into the gloom, bisected by a thick, twenty-foot standoff line painted in coarse, alchemical grit across the floor. On either side, massive iron cages—sheathed in spell-forged plate and braided abjuration matrices—turned unspeakable biological nightmares into sharp, shivering silhouettes. Backlighting inside the cells rendered every spine, every claw, and every twitching tendon in stark detail, allowing visual checks without bringing a throat within reaching distance of the bars.

Sable led the trio, her hobnailed boots striking the wet stone with military rhythm. She paused at the first heavy timber post, pressing two fingers into the soft lead seal on the log-board to verify the perimeter integrity. The seal was unbroken.

"Owner-access only," she reported, her voice flat and hollow in the narrow vault. She didn't look at the shadows inside the iron; her eyes were on the anchor-bolts holding the frames to the granite. "Twenty-foot standoff maintained around the clock. No stress testing. No touching the iron. Your doctrine, Banki. Executed without deviation."

Darric walked at Banki's shoulder, his hands folded behind his spine in a parade-rest posture designed to eliminate sudden, defensive movements. He didn't waste time scanning the monsters. His focus tracked the hinge-plates, the load-bearing welds on the latch-brackets, and the depth of the expansion pins driven into the stone floor. He was auditing the cage, not the inhabitant.

The first enclosure held the Reedsinger. Designated NMD-RS-01.

A foul, grey residue of burnt enchantment clung to the iron bars—the greasy stain of previous handlers who had tried and failed to force compliance through blunt mental domination. The beast itself radiated no visible magic. It possessed no glowing eyes, no aura of sulfur or frost. It sat huddled in the center of the cell, a gaunt, elongated horror wrapped in tatters of its own sloughed, translucent skin.

Yet its attention carried the physical pressure of an iron plate resting on the chest.

It did not pace the cell like a starved wolf. It did not hurl its bulk against the bars. It remained frozen, its twin obsidian eyes locked onto the three visitors. It tracked Banki's breath, measuring the expansion of his ribs. It mapped the microscopic muscle twitches in Darric's calves as the soldier shifted his stance. It listened, with terrifying precision, to the exact cadence of Sable's pulse pounding against her throat.

A raw, jagged transmission scraped against the inside of Banki's skull—a psychic bleed that felt like a rusted nail dragging across bone: *pattern... rhythm... gap... gap... gap...*

Banki hardened his mental shields, turning his surface thoughts to cold steel. "Learner-predator," he said, keeping his voice dead, stripped of all emotional cadence that the beast could analyze. "It isn't plotting a violent break. It’s mapping the mechanical lock of our attention. It’s calculating our nervous system's delay. Once it models a handler's tells—a blink, a hitch in breath, a preferred lead foot—it becomes statistically impossible to hold. It will strike straight through the lag in your reactions."

"White-noise metronomes," Darric cut in, his tone dropping to a dry, tactical drone. He knew the threat. "We mount clockwork noise-makers along the corridor. Shatter the acoustic mapping. We rotate handlers every shift and randomize approach vectors. Never let it learn the rhythm of a guard mount. If it can't predict the room, it can't launch."

Banki gave a tight, single nod. The protocol was sound. He broke eye contact with the Reedsinger, feeling the psychic pressure instantly snap toward the back of his neck like a hooked line. He ignored it. Next cage.

The Reed-Mirror Serpent. Designated UNQ-RS-02.

The cell appeared vacant at first glance—a massive terrarium of thick, alchemically tempered glass and heavy masonry. Banki launched a sharp mental probe through the pane, hunting for a life-signature.

The probe skated off.

The consciousness was frictionless. No resistance, no mental shield—merely an absolute lack of grip. Inside the enclosure, a localized refraction wash bent the air, wrapping the beast in a shifting skin of false light. Ambient illumination fractured across its scales. The reflections on the glass warped wildly, showing the serpent coiled in the far left corner for half a breath before the visual image snapped, revealing the monster pressed flat against the front pane, inches from Banki's face.

Looking at it directly triggered a sharp, nauseating pain behind the eye-sockets. The visual cortex rebelled against the impossible optical geometry.

*Don't look straight,* a cold, glassy thought drifted into the air, a predatory sneer.

"Slick mind," Banki noted, rubbing his temple to clear the spike of vertigo. "It projects a constant, localized refraction field. It’s waiting for the eye to blink—waiting for the handler to trust the illusion over the mass. If you open that door based on where you think it sits, it’s already coiled around your windpipe."

Next cell. The Shatterglass Lurker.

The entity undeniably filled the space behind the heavy iron bars, yet direct vision refused to lock onto its silhouette. Banki attempted to focus on its limbs, but his gaze slid off the sharp angles of its frame, violently pulling his eyes toward the empty corners of the cell. A walking blind spot—a predator evolved to erase itself from a prey animal's visual processing center.

"Perception trap," Darric spat, his jaw tight as he stared doggedly at the stone floor. "A nightmare for close-quarters squads. This breaks units, not doors. One guard screams it’s on the left, another swears it’s on the right, and the corporal steps closer to the bars to see who's lying. The confusion breeds panic. Panic breeds motion. Then the blood hits the floor."

"Keep it boring," Banki ordered, tracing the structural anchor-bolts instead of the shadow. "Automated chutes for raw meat. Zero direct contact. Excluded from standard sales. We only entertain offers for this asset if the coin is absurd, and only to vetted buyers who prove they understand cognitive hazards."

Further down the lane, the Displacer Beast paced in tight, hyper-efficient loops. Six heavily muscled legs padded silently over the stone. Its twin, spiked tentacles whipped the air with the crack of a slaver's lash. It paid the men no mind, marching its perimeter with the cold, calculating malice of an apex hunter denied a slaughter-field. The light around its flank lagged behind its motion, producing a stomach-churning trailing effect with every turn.

"Displacers punish routine harder than the Reedsinger," Darric warned, his voice grim with memories of jungle campaigns where squads were turned to mincemeat in their tents. "Randomize feeding times and entry lanes. Keep the inside of that cage sterile. No loose chains, no leather scraps, no targets. If you give it something to strike, it will map its reach. If you want auction stability, you train the staff to be flawless. You can't train the beast."

They passed through the quarantine airlock into the wet wing. Here, the air was thick with salt-damp, rotting kelp, and raw offal.

Kessa, the beastmaster recruit, stood on the elevated gantry above the Transition Tank—a massive, sunken pool of brackish water carved directly into the bedrock. She held a long, steel-reinforced pole. An inert bait bundle—brined meat soaked in fish oil, devoid of any fresh blood splash that might trigger a frenzy—hung from a cable inches above the dark water.

A dark, torpedo-like silhouette rose from the depths without a wave. It did not thrash. It did not surge. It glided upward—a shadow separating itself from the black water. It brushed the bait with the hardened edge of its snout, testing the density, verifying the contact.

A single, explosive snap of jaws sheared the meat clean from the line. The teeth cut through dense sinew without a sound. The beast sank back into the murky depths without a single splash disturbing the surface.

"Trainable," Banki murmured, watching the clean, disciplined execution. "Offer the hatchlings choices. Build the holding tanks with varied micro-climates—warm dry hides, wet moss banks, shallow waders. Let them choose their territory before we assign them a tactical role. A comfortable asset is a predictable asset."

Before the shift-change, Sable assembled the staff in the preparation hall. Thirty guards, handlers, and runners stood in rigid ranks. Sable stood on the stone mounting block, positioned so every eyes could see her empty, gloved hands. She projected absolute, ruthless authority.

"Curiosity kills operations," she announced, her voice flat, carrying the cold finality of an iron door slamming shut. "If you gossip about the inventory in the low vaults, you become inventory for our rivals. You make yourselves targets for syndicate snatch-squads looking for a weak mouth. If you talk in the taverns, you get one warning. The second time, you are reassigned to the latrine drains until your skin rots off."

She paced the line, her cold gaze locking onto the younger recruits. "If a buyer tries to pump you for details, you recite the printed manifest. You know nothing beyond the lot numbers. If one of Banki's shadows tries to pump you for details... you shut them down the same. My floor. My rules. Your loyalty is to the iron of this facility. Do we understand each other?"

Silence held the hall. The policy was no longer words on paper; it had become an iron weight resting on every neck. Banki watched from the doorway, his face expressionless, thoroughly satisfied with the cold efficiency. The clever ones stopped trying to be clever. They became cogs in Sable's machine. Exactly as required.

## Crown Citizenship

The kingdom inaugurated itself not with the raising of a blood-soaked banner or the crowning of a monarch, but with a ledger.

Mara's iron-bound book lay open on the high salvage desk, its vellum pages thick as cured hide, its spine reinforced with heavy brass straps. The ink she had brewed that morning stank of iron gall, bone-ash, and something far older—a sharp, copper stench that hung in the back of the throat. Banki had bled into the crucible before dawn, three precise cuts across his left palm, letting his blood pulse into the pigment while the batch was still boiling. The magic threaded through the ink as dye threads through water: invisible, absolute, and permanent.

When a citizen signed their name in that ink, the act closed an metaphysical circuit. Their true name—written in their own hand, pressed into parchment mixed with blood and bone-ash—anchored a locator thread to their soul. The magic did not compel. It did not enslave the mind, erase a memory, or force a slavish oath of fealty. That was the crude work of street-tyrants and slave-lords.

Instead, Banki had woven a soul-address into the ink—a locator thread engineered to mimic the subtle psychic warmth of home, like the smell of a hearth fire after months on a frozen road. The magic settled into the signatory's identity—a quiet, thrumming beacon bound to their true name, remaining active until extreme distance, absolute terror, or death severed the line. A modified Asmodean contract working, designed to track rather than to possess.

Tovin Vale—a sharp-eyed, narrow-shouldered boy pulled from the Belowmarket fighting pits—sat on an oak crate near the kitchen hearth. He was systematically testing the mechanical resistance of a brass latch with a worn, child-sized screwdriver. Mara had written his name for him that morning, his fingers too scarred and stiff to hold the quill. He had watched the ink dry on the vellum with the hyper-vigilant suspicion of a rat watching a trap.

Tovin did not look up as Banki approached.

"If I get lost," Tovin asked, his voice scraped raw of childhood innocence, sounding like a veteran scout checking his kit, "does my name in that book find me?"

Banki paused, calibrating his words. Children detected lies long before adults parsed the subtext. Lie to the boy now, and the anchor was worthless.

"If the thread remains whole," Banki said, keeping his tone flat, stripped of pity. "If your name stays your own, and your mind remains intact. The magic allows me to follow a thread through the city’s mud. A light in the dark. But it cannot spin a new line after the world has severed the old one. Heavy lead-warding or deep salt-water will drown the signal."

Tovin digested this, his screwdriver pausing on the brass screw. He pulled his narrow shoulders inward—a street-rat's guard posture. "So it isn't a collar."

"No," Banki stated. "The magic carries no command. It forces no duty. It demands no blood-debt. It finds the door. It is not an iron ring around your neck."

The sharp, heavy strike of Sable's boots against the flagstones presaged her arrival. She strode into the hall, the heat from the subterranean kitchens doing nothing to thaw the ice in her eyes. She pointed a gloved finger at the open ledger, her voice low, vibrating with an anger that silenced the room.

"You bled into the ink." Not a question; the copper stench had betrayed him. "You wove a leash into the parchment they sign. Every desperate soul who puts their name in that book carries your thread without knowing the price."

Banki leaned back against the granite wall, his face a mask, though a flicker of irritation stirred his chest. "If I wanted mindless thralls, Sable, I have far more violent tools than an inkwell. I could rewrite their loyalties while they sleep in their cots."

"That isn't an answer!" Sable snapped, stepping into his space, completely unbothered by the shadows swirling around his cloak. "That's a threat wrapped in restraint. You hid tracking magic in a *voluntary* oath! *Voluntary*, Banki! The word is a lie when the ink is rigged!"

"It is tactical doctrine," Banki countered, his voice dropping to a dangerous whisper. "The world outside these gates views them as meat for the butcher. I am ensuring the meat carries a beacon."

Mara, sitting behind the ledger, paid the escalating argument no mind. Her silver nib scratched across the heavy vellum with cold, administrative precision.

"Crown Citizenship," Mara declared, her voice cutting through the tension like an iron wedge. "Structured on the precedents of the Asmodean Pact, adapted for mortal survival. Voluntary oaths only. Named, codified rights. Named, absolute duties. The Crown guarantees rations, fair wages, fortified shelter, medical treatment, and an absolute, unbreakable prohibition against forced mind-alteration for every citizen."

She looked up from the book, her quill poised. Her gaze shifted between Sable and Banki. "If the locator ink stays, it is disclosed. Page one. Directly above the signature line. They are told what the ink does before the nib touches the paper." The quill tapped once against the brass lip of the inkwell. "Or I burn the batch."

Sable turned from Banki, leaning over the desk to scan the wet ink. "Add a clause. Right there, beneath the medical provisions. Forbidding experimental laboratory work on Crown Citizens. No alchemical trials. No testing new ward-matrices on the staff. Even if they offer verbal consent out of fear or hunger. *Especially* then. Children under Crown protection are never leverage. We do not use them against heroes, we do not use them against our enemies, and we do not use them to prove our own righteousness to the city."

Mara nodded, her nib flying across the vellum. She completed the Crown Contract before the morning bell rang. The text offered no easy pardons for past crimes, nor did it paint a delusional utopia. Treason carried swift, brutal execution. The oaths retained their absolute, magical weight. But the ink bound the Crown as fiercely as it bound the citizen: rations preceded labor, fair coin preceded luxury, rescue preceded punishment. No citizen of Sablehook would ever be spent as tactical fodder.

And on the first page, in Mara's sharp script, the mandatory disclosure:

*The ink of this ledger holds a locator thread bound to your true name. It enables the Crown to track your signal. It does not enable the Crown to command your will. If you demand unbound ink, request it. Your citizenship will not be denied.*

At the foot of the document, framed by Mara's precise flourishes, a single line anchored the philosophy of Sablehook:

*The Crown demands service. It does not own the soul.*

By dusk, the heavy ledger faced its first true test.

Hest—a woman pulled from the warehouse ruins who had eaten two full meals in the hall that day—approached the desk. She stared at the open vellum, at the cold disclosure on page one, at the dark, copper-smelling ink in the well. Her hands shook so violently she had to interlock her fingers to hide the tremor. Her eyes kept darting toward the iron gates.

She refused Crown Citizenship.

She demanded her three days' guaranteed exit-wage, a wrapped ration from the kitchen, and the immediate opening of the gate. She wanted back out into the mud of the streets.

The ancient, predatory instinct roared inside Banki’s chest—a violent, tyrannical urge born of his absolute power to hoard the vulnerable. To protect by force. To cage the weak behind iron doors, enforcing safety through total dominance so the city's meat-grinder could never chew them up again. He could freeze her nervous system with a single word. He could rewrite her terror, forcing her to see the stone walls as a sanctuary instead of a cell.

Sable stood rigid near the hearth, watching him. She didn't reach for a blade. She simply watched the war raging behind Banki's eyes.

Mara, unreadable, turned the page past the Voluntary Oath, ready to record a departure.

Banki forced his fists to uncurl. The physical strain of holding back his magic made his finger-joints pop. He exhaled a long, ragged breath, violently shoving the tyrannical hoarding instinct back into the dark vault of his mind. He locked the door on it.

"Pay her," Banki rasped, his voice sounding like grinding iron.

Mara handed over the silver coins. The kitchen team packed the bread in waxed paper. Hest departed with the coin, the food, and zero strands of magic bound to her name in the ledger. The heavy iron gate slammed shut behind her, the metallic boom echoing hollowly through the damp courtyard.

Banki stared at the barred entry, his jaw set so hard his molars ground together. "She will be dead within a month. The Rot-Tooth syndicate runs her old alley. They will smell the silver."

"Then she dies free," Sable said softly, the edge finally leaving her voice. She walked over, standing at his shoulder in the dim light. "A room you aren't allowed to leave is a cage, Banki. No matter how much bread is on the table. Especially when a man with power stands over you and calls it mercy."

Banki turned away from the gate, looking back into the deep vaults of the compound, listening to the muffled, rhythmic pacing of the beasts locked in the subterranean cells. The teeth of his new kingdom were growing sharper by the hour. Now, they simply had to learn who they were permitted to tear apart.



# Chapter 6

## The Weight of the Crown

The air inside the Gull & Gasket’s private back-room smelled of rancid tallow, damp oak, and the sharp, metallic tang of cold-blooded treason. A single lumen-globe hung from the low rafters, its tarnished brass cage black with soot, casting harsh, unforgiving light across the scarred table. The timber was salvage—a massive deck-plank ripped from the corpse of a sunken naval dreadnought, stained black with decades of spilled rum, dried sea-blood, and clandestine bargains.

Mara sat across the timber. Her spine remained rigidly straight, her ink-stained fingers folded over a heavy, iron-clasped ledger. She wore the drab grey wool of a dock-clerk, a drab skin chosen to dissolve into Blackwater Quay’s background noise. It failed entirely to soften the predatory stillness of an elite handler. Mara had outlived three grand-dukes and two guild massacres by knowing precisely when to hold her tongue and when to slide a stiletto between rib-joints.

"Possibly," Mara said.

The word dropped into the quiet like a lead sinker into a deep well. Heavy. Unyielding. Offering no purchase for comfort.

"That does not trouble you?" Banki asked. His voice was a flatline, testing the tensile strength of her resolve.

"Less than a crown that calls a man's refusal treason."

The Crown Citizen Contract rested between them on the dreadnought wood—a thick sheet of cured vellum, stiff and arrogant, bearing the heavy, crimson wax seal of the Capital Chancery at the bottom margin. The text mapped out the exact geometry of absolute submission: exorbitant port tithes on all salvaged stock, mandatory conscription quotas for the coastal fleet, and the immediate, compulsory surrender of any unsanctioned arcana or wild-casters. It was bureaucratic ironwork—a legal vice engineered to crush the independent captains of Blackwater Quay into obedient dust.

Banki dragged the vellum across the table. The rasp of dry hide against rough oak was loud as a blade clearing a scabbard. Unstorking an iron-glass inkwell, the bitter reek of crushed gall-nuts and fermented vitriol cut through the room's stale oil. He dipped his iron quill. The nib clicked against the rim, holding a fat, shivering drop of black ink.

He brought the point down, three inches below the Chancery's designated signature mark. The nib bit deep, scratching violently into the fibers. He offered no wordy preamble. He drafted no polite grievance for a court magistrate. He added a single, jagged line of script, driving the point so hard the ink bled through the hide like a fresh wound.

*Refusal of the Crown is not betrayal.*

He laid the quill down. The black ink glistened under the lumen-globe—a ragged scar sliced across the kingdom’s absolute mandate.

Mara watched the nib finish, reading the upside-down script without moving an eyelid. Her jaw did not set; her throat did not twitch. She simply gave a single, icy nod, accepting the lethal reality of the stroke. Her first rebellion against the throne carried no heraldry. It was merely the first strike in a very quiet, very bloody war.

## The Eldritch Hour

Stillness mimicked a cage too closely for comfort. When the tavern floorboards above finally stopped groaning under the boots of drunk sailors, when Blackwater Quay’s endless reek of fish-scale and night-soil subsided into a fitful slumber, Banki went down into the dark.

The cellar beneath the Gull & Gasket waited in pitch silence.

Solitary training was his only true refusal. Refusal to sleep while an ambitious rival might measure his neck for iron. Refusal to let his body devolve into passive waiting. Refusal to learn, when the cold steel was already in his guts, that he had left a weakness unpatched.

He snapped his fingers, sparking a pinhead flame that lit the subterranean vault. The yellow glare caught the marrow-runes he had cut into the floor flagstones over six brutal weeks. Ground from the scorched femurs of predatory drakes and tempered with quicksilver, the sigils spiraled across the floor in lethal defensive arcs. Bone-dust vectors marked out clean kill-lanes. Blood-iron nails had been hammered into the load-bearing pillars, anchoring the volatile magic straight into the bedrock. Three mechanical deadbolts and two biometric blood-latches held the ironwood door. Sable’s acoustic baffles—compressed cork, woven ghost-silk, and deadened air-pockets—sheathed the stone, sealing the vault in a tomb of silence.

The room met standard parameters for safety—a meaningless metric. Safety was a fantasy sold to weak men to keep them docile. Absolute, overwhelming dominance was the only currency that held value.

Banki stepped into the exact center of the bone-dust circle.

He raised his right hand. Magic answered instantly, raw and violent. The spell coalesced in his palm—a black-violet mass of kinetic malice that stripped the heat from the surrounding air. The Weave Spike twisted into the shape of a screaming, hollow-eyed skull, shedding a sharp reek of scorched copper and ozone. He bypassed the safety limiters, altering the formula mid-manifestation, burning structural stability to maximize sheer armor-piercing velocity.

He released it.

The bolt crossed the vault in a fraction of a heartbeat, slamming into the target-wardstone anchored in the far wall. The impact detonated like a siege-engine firing inside a tomb. The massive block of granite shuddered to its roots, glowing a dull, angry cherry-red as the kinetic and arcane violence was siphoned off by the blood-iron anchors and dumped harmlessly into the earth.

Again.

He drew mana faster, ignoring the sharp, electrical burn tearing along his forearms. He compressed the screaming visage of the spell, crushing the volatile energy into a dense, silent needle. The Tri-Weave clicked into alignment—a ruthless fusion of three contradictory disciplines. Arcane geometry dictated the rigid flight path. Sovereign alignment asserted absolute right-of-way, stripping the target's natural spell-shedding properties. Psionic force braided the dual currents into an adaptable, piercing drill.

He released it.

The lance punched straight into the glowing red crater left by the previous strike. The Tri-Weave adapted dynamically to the stone's thermal density on contact, shifting frequencies in a microsecond. The strike drove a smoking, two-inch hole straight through twenty-four inches of warded granite. The noisy spectacle of the skull was gone, replaced by pure, terrifying penetration. A violent answer for heavy plate, drake-hide, or vault doors.

Again.

For two unbroken hours, the cellar filled with the stench of scorched stone and the sour, battery-acid taste of spent power. Banki adjusted his footwork continuously, shifting his center of mass. He engaged full tell-suppression—swallowing incantations, killing micro-gestures, forcing high-tier magic to detonate without a fraction of warning. Every tweak was cataloged in his mental ledger alongside every other lesson the world had beaten into his flesh. Burning fatigue built in his deltoids and hamstrings—a heavy, toxic sludge he systematically bled off through Iron Halo thresholds. He monitored his heart rate, holding it at a steady, rhythmic drive.

Then came the fine work.

He glided to a scarred workbench at the periphery of the array. A set of steel picks, a double-edged throwing dagger, and a sealed glass vial of black ink sat on the raw pine.

He extended his mind. A telekinetic vise clamped around all three objects at once. They lifted, floating suspended at eye level. This was micro-torque manipulation—a brutal departure from the heavy impact of the Weave Spike. It required splitting his consciousness into three independent processing threads: weight, rotation, and fine surface pressure.

One thread worked the tension-hook through the tumblers of an invisible lock, applying precisely three ounces of upward torque while feathering a pick. A second thread turned the throwing knife on its axis, maintaining a slow, fluid spin while holding the needle-point dead center on a speck of dust drifting through the damp air. The third thread cradled the glass vial in a perfect, hovering arc, adjusting force so precisely that the black fluid inside never rippled against the glass.

Beneath the steady breathing, a shadow stirred in his deep memory.

The old room rose to the surface. Not as a thought, but as a suffocating sensory breach. The sharp, chemical stink of carbolic acid and rust burning his nostrils. The cold, unyielding iron grid biting into his naked spine. The glaring blue-white glare of the alchemical lamps blinding his eyes. Surgeon-Prefect Vane standing over his restrained form, silver calipers in hand, evaluating Banki's exposed ribcage not as a child's flesh, but as volatile raw stock.

*“Remarkable neural tolerance,”* Vane’s voice echoed, razor-clean and hollowed of humanity. *“Administer the secondary paralytic. We will map the psychic overload limit before the heart ruptures.”*

A specimen had no vote. A specimen owned no flesh. A specimen waited in agony until the men with knives decided the trial was done. The thick leather strap biting into his throat. The cold needle sliding beneath his collarbone, pumping liquid fire directly into his spine.

He had sworn long ago that no trial would ever end until he held the blade.

The glass vial cracked.

The sharp *snap* shattered the vault's quiet like a pistol shot. A hairline fracture spider-webbed across the glass, and a fat bead of black ink welled along the seam. His telekinetic grip had spasmed around the dagger the instant the memory breached his focus, transferring a micro-ton of pressure to the vial. A minor error.

Unforgivable.

Instinct demanded immediate, violent self-punishment. Add ten pounds of telekinetic load. Extend the session by two hours. Repeat the drill until the nerves, the magic, and the memory bowed to absolute command. The urge to break his own bone to enforce discipline burned hot in his throat.

He held the weeping vial in the air for five long seconds. A single drop of ink fell, hitting the pine bench with a wet tap.

He forced his hands to lower.

The restraint chafed like raw iron on open flesh. Letting a flaw pass unpunished felt like leaving a city gate wide to an army. But the crack in the glass had come not from a soft hand, but from an old flaw: treating his own mind as an enemy to be beaten into submission without considering the stress-fractures left in the weapon. A blade honed too hard simply shattered under strain.

The pick, the dagger, and the ruined vial settled softly onto the bench.

For ten seconds, Banki let the room hold absolute silence. He tracked his breath. In. Out.

The vault walls did not close in. The masonry remained dry stone, refusing to dissolve into the rusted iron cages of his youth. Above, Edric Mallon’s tavern slept its greasy sleep, the heavy floorboards groaning under the weight of slumbering cutthroats. Beyond that, Sable managed her ledgers, salt-lines, and the fragile lives of people coming to view these walls as a fortress. The psychic weight of the city pressed down—real, heavy, and grounded in cold dirt.

He took a square of coarse burlap and scrubbed the pine bench, dragging the cloth back and forth until the black ink was forced deep under his fingernails. Magic could have erased the stain in a breath. Manual labor left a physical record. It proved he occupied the room on his own terms.

He closed his eyes and unlatched his inner sight.

The physical vault dissolved into a complex lattice of lingering energy signatures. Old wards hummed with a thick amber pulse. Force-scars from yesterday’s Weave Spikes burned like deep blue bruises against the granite. Sable’s salt-lines, buried deep within the mortar, thrummed with a steady, defensive white hum. The brutal impact-points of every Tri-Weave strike hung in the air like glowing smoke. Rooms lied after violence; power always left footprints. Reading them faster than an invading squad meant the difference between setting an ambush and walking into a kill-box.

He refined the tracking technique until gray dawn light scraped through the iron street grates high overhead. As the first cart wheels rumbled above, he brushed bone-dust from his knees, verified the lock-tumblers, and pulled his psychic baffles tight around his mind.

Upstairs, Edric Mallon was already burning coffee with impressive dedication.

The tavern master stood behind the long bar, a greasy leather apron wrapped around his gut, pouring boiling water over grounds that smelled like scorched grain and beetles. Edric slid a chipped clay mug across the counter. He caught sight of the black ink rammed under Banki’s nails and swallowed whatever question he had primed. He picked up his own cup, holding it in both scarred hands, though the saucer rattled against the wood anyway.

"Rough shift?" Edric muttered.

"Useful one."

Banki drank the bitter brew, letting the heat scorch away the lingering taste of ozone and dried blood. Three hours of sleep, four of discipline. Sleep kept the body from making fatal errors; discipline reminded the flesh that submission was a death sentence.

## The Caravan Launches

The Sablehook staging yard drowned in predawn mist—a thick, coastal wall rolling off Blackwater Quay that reeked of salt-damp, rotting kelp, and old copper. The one-week acquisition run operated under strict military doctrine. No public fanfare. No inventory details leaked to street runners. A dull commercial transport posture, executed with the cold precision of a black-budget raid.

The yard hummed with disciplined movement.

Kestrel hovered over the heavy Expedition Dossier, which lay flat across a stack of sealed ironwood crates. Her scout-barding fit tight across her shoulders, the matte-black hide treated to swallow stray light and deaden the rustle of leather. She traced a gloved talon along the mapped route, her jaw set hard. Beneath her sleeves, the raised, scar-thickened tracks of her grafted bone-blades waited for a signal.

Vesper paced the outer perimeter, his eyes continuously reading the fog. He moved with the oily grace of a retired Crown assassin, a pair of custom repeater crossbows riding high on his thighs, loaded with heavy armor-piercing quarrel-heads.

Gravewire inspected the steel axle-tree bolts on the heavy transport wagons. His massive frame dominated the yard—less a man and more a siege-golem forged of scarred muscle, iron pins, and deep-cut marrow-tattoos. A immovable anchor ready to drop on any obstacle.

Tally, the disgraced guild clerk who viewed numbers with religious terror, shuffled rapidly through a deck of blank customs manifests, muttering under his breath as he forged the bureaucratic camouflage to fool Crown inspectors.

And Kessa stood before the six dire rhinos.

The beasts were siege-engines of flesh. Each stood six feet at the humped shoulder, armored in thick, overlapping plates of hide that resembled rusted iron plate. They blew thick plumes of white vapor into the freezing air, shifting their massive weight, their iron-shod hooves striking the cobbles with bone-jarring thuds. The thick stink of damp leather, dung, and aggressive musk filled the yard, cutting through the salt-fog. Kessa ran her bare hands over the heavy leather traces, testing the tension of the iron buckles. She maintained a quiet pain-sharing link with the beasts, keeping their rising anxiety clamped down before it could turn to rage.

Fifteen veterans. Three heavy, steel-ribbed wagons. Six dire rhinos.

Banki stood on the high loading dock, watching the gears of his machine turn. It was time to link the network.

He closed his eyes and built a telepathic pulse. He cast no open shout. He forged a single, sharp needle of thought, compressed it into a high-frequency burst, and drove it simultaneously into the minds of his squad leads.

*Status. Single line. Failures only.*

The mental network flexed under the load. Reports returned in a rapid, disciplined volley, sorted instantly by his cognitive filters.

*Route green,* Kestrel transmitted, her mental signature sharp as a razor-edge. *First leg Brinewood border. ETA locked.*

*Counter-surveillance green,* Vesper sent—a smooth, oily slide across the bond. *No tail attached to the gate. Two watchers at the split-latch displaying passive count behavior. Low-tier gang runners. No pursuit vectors.*

*Perimeter green,* Gravewire thrummed—a heavy, metallic pulse like a hammer hitting an anvil. *Gate authority verified. No forced contact.*

*Documentation dull,* Tally reported—a nervous flutter of numbers quickly suppressed. *Cover holds. We are hauling low-grade textile scrap.*

*Containment SOP active,* Kessa finished, her connection thrumming with the heavy, rhythmic heartbeat of the beasts. *Traces fitted. Empty cradles prepped for live stock. Preservative jars sealed. Zero stress spikes on the rhinos.*

All green. No criticals.

Sable stepped onto the dock. She wore her old field leathers—the scuffed gear from their days in the deep dark—her black hair bound high with a leather thong. She offered no soft farewells to the morning. An iron-bound ledger was tucked beneath her left arm. She would hold Blackwater Quay, managing the auction floor, paying the bribes, and keeping her boot on the city's throat while he drove into the wild.

"One week," Banki told her, his voice low enough that the fog drank the words before they reached the team. "Brinewood border, then the Shattercliff gap, then the turn-back. Keep the clerk's desk operating as logged. Two signatures on all high-coin paper. Maintain observe-only on the Wakeblack leads. Do not strike if they touch the perimeter."

"The floor stays boring and absolute," Sable answered. Her fingers tapped a slow, steady cadence against the ledger's iron binding. "Nobody moves without my stamp. It will be here when you return. Bigger. Colder."

Banki gave a single nod. He swung down from the dock and mounted the bench of the lead wagon. The ironwood seat groaned under his weight. He took the thick leather reins, feeling the cold, hostile tension of the rhinos at the other end.

He cracked the leather.

The rhinos leaned into the traces. Massive shoulders bunched. Iron traces pulled taut with a sharp, metallic screech. The heavy caravan rolled, iron-clad hooves striking the cobbles like a muffled drumbeat. The thick wheels ground gravel to dust as they passed through Sablehook’s outer gates, leaving Blackwater Quay to rot quietly in its sea-fog.

The city fell away past the final guard-tower, and the minds went with it.

One moment—sixty, seventy cognitive sparks burned bright against his awareness. The baker’s wife tallying grain-sacks, her arithmetic tight and sharp. A dock-hand nursing a crushed thumb, the throb of it pulsing dull and red in the static. Edric Mallon behind his bar, mug hot against his calluses, worrying about his tap-house stock. The dense, familiar pressure of human thought—warm, chaotic, relentless—pressing against his skull like a pulse he had learned to navigate by feel.

The lead wagon cleared the last slum-shackle.

The sparks died. All of them. In a single instant.

The inside of his skull went dead silent with a violence that made his teeth snap together. His ears rang with a high, thin whine—the phantom protest of a sense stripped of its medium. His balance pitched hard on the wooden bench, a sudden, sickening vertigo as the invisible framework he had leaned on for weeks vanished, leaving him tilting into the void. The absence was not quiet. It was *deafening*—the pressurized silence of a deep vault after an explosion, heavy enough to set his jaw aching and force his hands to lock onto the reins until his knuckles turned white.

He blinked twice, fighting the sensation that his vision had gone flat. The fog-slick road ahead looked stripped of depth, like a bad painting missing its shadow.

The rhinos kept pulling. The traces screeched. Mud sucked at the heavy wheels. And nothing else. No warmth. No hum of fear, greed, or ambition pressing against his mind. Only the vast, indifferent hostility of a landscape that did not think, did not care, and did not register his existence.

Something primal in his brainstem, older than language, read the silence as an ambush.

The reins stabilized. His breathing flattened. By the second mile, the void had compressed into a dull, thrumming ache behind his eyes—the psychic phantom of an amputated limb. The wild offered its own static eventually. Thin, alien signatures: the frantic pulse of a vole bolting through scrub, the icy patience of a hawk riding a thermal, the slow, vegetative hum of ancient roots drinking salt-water. Nothing like the dense architecture of the city. Noise without grammar.

Caravan discipline calcified immediately into brutal routine. Rise an hour before dawn. Audit the leather traces for rot-cracks. Check the glass preservation jars for micro-fractures. Maintain total silence for the first mile of travel to establish a clean auditory baseline. The grinding monotony stripped away city politics, leaving only mud, cold iron, and the mechanical drive of the acquisition squad pushing into the dark.

## The Brinewood

By the third day, the Brinewood swallowed them whole.

The forest did not grow; it festered. Twisted, black-barked trunks the color of dried blood choked the trail, their gnarled canopy weaving a ceiling so dense it strangled the sun, dropping the forest floor into a perpetual, blood-tinted twilight. The air beneath the boughs was heavy and hot, reeking of wet rot and the sharp, vinegar stink of corrosive moss that rusted the wagon bolts on contact. Razor-bramble—thick as a man's wrist and spiked with iron-hard thorns—coiled across the roots like sleeping vipers, ready to flay the legs of any careless scout.

The caravan slowed to an agonizing crawl. The dire rhinos snorted violently, their small red eyes rolling in their sockets as they caught the scent of apex predators bleeding through the vegetation. The corrosive moss hissed softly as the ironwood wheels crushed it, releasing small clouds of choking green gas. The weight of the forest settled over the crew like a wet shroud. The woods were old, hostile, and starving.

Deep within the gloom, a spike of disciplined emotion shot through the bond—Kestrel’s signature, tight with controlled adrenaline.

*Target. East flank. Heavy. Reedsinger.*

Kestrel dropped from a high branch without a sound, her boots finding a mossy root. She signaled her vector marks, confirming the line.

The Reedsinger. A serpentine terror logged in Chancery bestiaries as highly lethal and immensely valuable on the black-market auction circuit. The manifest demanded a live capture. Through the tangled briars, Banki caught its outline.

Its fourteen-foot body lay coiled across a flat, sun-baked slab of granite that had punched through the canopy. Its scales shifted in a mesmerizing optical cascade—transitioning from a dull, mottled emerald that matched the rot-moss to a brilliant, mirror-finish silver as it registered micro-vibrations in the dirt. The beast carried no venom. Its jaws possessed little crushing power. It required neither. The massive, ribbed throat-sac pulsing beneath its lower jaw was a biological engine of destruction. It could shatter granite, turn internal organs to jelly, and reduce a man’s brain to liquid paste with a focused sonic scream. The rock beneath it was ringed by thirty yards of pulverized stone and splintered timber—the debris of its last kill.

"Standard iron nets will trigger the throat-sac the moment they hit the scales," Vesper whispered, crouching behind a root cluster with his crossbows raised, though they were useless here. "We need it locked down before it knows it’s hunted. One scream in this timber, and the reverberation will crush our ribcages."

"Hold line. Maintain visual. Do not engage."

Banki stepped clear of the wagons. He pulled the ambient shadows from the underbrush, weaving them into a void-cloak that erased his silhouette from the light. He initiated the internal shifts. He hollowed his bones, pulling the marrow density into an arcane reserve, swallowing the sudden wave of nausea. He compressed his muscle fibers, shrinking his physical profile. He severed his cognitive weight-anchor, letting gravity release its grip on his boots.

The heavy air carried him. He drifted forward, floating three feet above the rotting moss, passing over trip-vines and dry twigs without disturbing a single molecule of air.

He hovered directly above the Reedsinger’s armored head.

The beast's mind held no words. When Banki touched its surface, it thrummed with raw, sensory impulses: *Sun-rock. Belly-heat. Meat-hunger. Air-shift. Scale-lock. Prepare-throat.* The silver scales flashed brighter, achieving a blinding mirror sheen in the dim light. The ribbed throat-sac expanded violently, drawing in a massive chest of air. It registered the shift in the environment, even if its eyes could not track the shadow hovering above its crown.

Banki unleashed the Tri-Weave.

He cast no three separate spells. He deployed a single, unified metaphysical strike engineered for absolute subjugation.

First, the arcane formula struck. It mathematically altered the space around the Reedsinger’s skull, locking the local air into a frictionless vacuum, stripping the beast of its physical evasion angles.

Second, the sovereign alignment crushed its rising panic. Banki drove an absolute, cold tranquility into the cracks of its terror—the unyielding command of a god demanding total stillness, overriding the reptilian fight-or-flight reflex.

Third, the psionic fracture severed its motor control. A telekinetic vise—honed in the cellar on glass vials and phantom locks—clamped around the beast’s jaw and the muscular resonance chambers of its throat.

The air in its lungs froze into an invisible cage.

The Reedsinger violently tried to force breath into its throat-sac to scream. The massive neck muscles spasmed against the constraint. The command from its brain met a wall of absolute static. The lower jaw refused to drop. The throat-sac remained crushed, bulging against the invisible pressure. Banki felt the strain down his own spine, micro-fibers tearing in his deltoids as he matched the beast's immense physical power with pure, focused will.

The silver scales rapidly lost their shine, fading back to a dull, lifeless emerald. Its slit pupils rolled back, fighting a losing battle against the enforced tranquility. Consciousness dropped from the violent drive of a predator into the dark stupor of an induced coma. The massive body went limp, sliding an inch down the warm granite.

"Target secured."

Banki's voice broke the quiet—dry, raspy, and devoid of heat.

Kessa moved in with the heavy capture team, executing containment SOP with practiced efficiency. Gravewire hauled a reinforced, lead-lined transport cradle into the clearing, his boots sinking into the pulverized rock. Kessa slapped a heavy alchemical muzzle over the beast's jaw, mechanically locking the throat-sac in a compressed state. Rubberized seals were verified; zero direct skin-contact permitted. The beast was logged on Tally’s manifest as LIVE-01—a prime asset destined for Sablehook’s quarantine vaults.

Banki dropped his shadow-cloak, restoring his natural mass. His boots hit the damp earth with a heavy thud. A dull, thrumming ache bloomed behind his eyes—the cost of driving the Tri-Weave without vocal components.

"Load it," Banki rasped, turning back to the trail. "Next target."

## Campfire in the Dark

Camp Eight sat at the high border of the Brinewood, perched on a rocky shelf where the canopy finally gave way to cold stone. The air here was thin, smelling of exposed granite and the sharp, clean bite of high altitude.

The fire burned low, fueled by dry cedar to eliminate smoke.

Kestrel sat at the light’s edge, silent and hyper-focused. She was breaking down her blade-mounts and foreclaws with an oiled cloth. The steady *scrape, click, slide* of steel served as a rhythmic counterpoint to the howling wind in the peaks. From the wagons came the soft, nerve-straining clatter of glass preservation jars settling, the heavy, drugged breathing of LIVE-01 in its lead cradle, and the restless shifting of the rhinos on the cold stone.

The firelight touched Banki's face without warming his skin. The cold road routine held the camp in its grip. Out in the dark, Gravewire walked the perimeter, his heavy boots deliberately masking the subtle snaps of dry brush. Near the hearth, Tally updated his manifests by lantern-light, keeping the paper trail dull and unshakeable. Kessa completed her rounds, inspecting the salt-lines and containment bolts around the live wagons.

"The Shattercliff pass," Kestrel said, her voice rough, scraped raw by cold air and three days of strict silence. She didn't look up from her claws, applying rust-inhibitor to the steel. "The gap through the Ironhorn Spur cuts two days off the return leg. Minimizes exposure with LIVE-01 and spares the rhinos the long climb over the teeth."

She paused, testing the edge of a claw against her leather-wrapped thumb.

"The locals won't touch it. They swear the gap is haunted by things that drink ambient light and remember your true name."

"Magical fauna," Banki replied, staring into the embers. "Border folk rarely separate supernatural phenomena from natural apex predators. They lack the academic taxonomy for anomalous beasts, so they build ghost stories."

"Can you verify?" Kestrel asked, finally looking up. Her golden eyes caught the firelight, glinting with predatory focus.

Banki closed his eyes and cast his psychic net out into the dark.

He bypassed the camp perimeter, driving past the standard tactical boundaries. He sacrificed near-sight clarity for distance, pushing his awareness up the sheer incline into the wind-scoured throat of the Ironhorn Spur.

The mountain night opened in his mind. Small, bright signatures of life: insects dormant in stone cracks, the sudden panic of a high-altitude vole, the cold hunger of an owl gliding through the pines. He drove deeper, into the narrow gap itself.

The noise died.

No human minds waited in the pass. No bandit ambush prepared cold steel. No necromantic void radiated the freezing static of undead.

Yet at the absolute limit of his reach, something hovered. Not a mind in any biological sense. It was the psychic footprint of something that had once possessed consciousness, or perhaps an entity that fed directly on thought itself. An echo. A complex, self-replicating pattern residing in the void between memory and absence. It felt like standing on the lip of an endless drop into an icy ocean. Hungry, ancient, and terrifyingly still. It consumed the ambient mana around it, creating a localized void.

He snapped the telepathic link back hard before the entity could trace the strand back to his brain.

He opened his eyes. A sharp, cold smile touched his mouth—devoid of humor.

"Something occupies the pass," Banki said softly over the fire’s crackle. "Not a ghost. An anomaly that leaves a massive psychic imprint while lacking a readable brain. A localized void-predator."

Kestrel’s jaw tightened. She knew that smile. The optional route had just become mandatory.

"I’ll prep the gear," Kestrel said, sliding her final claw into its mount with a hard click. "Heavy flares and triple salt-lines on all transport cradles."

"Do it. We move at dawn."

Banki looked past the camp boundary into the black maw of the peaks. The darkness waited—ancient, cold, and lethal. It held no terror for him. It was merely unharvested stock, another asset to be cataloged, bound, and eventually turned against the Crown.



# Chapter 7

## The Brain and the Binding

The Pink Annex derived its name from the pale, rose-hued crust of oxidation flaking across the heavy brass bulkheads—a chemical scar left by decades of continuous alchemical scrubbing. The isolation bay reeked of that sterile decay: a harsh, metallic tang laced with the biting sting of ozone and absolute containment. Every four seconds, the primary air-cyclers struck with a dull, hydraulic thud, dragging out the stale atmosphere and forcing in a fresh, hyper-oxygenated mixture.

Mirell stood beside Crate 12, frozen as stone. Her right palm hovered an inch above the manual kill-feed actuator. She wore a heavy Class-Four hazard rig, the vulcanized rubber and lead-weave mesh completely hiding her features behind a dark, polarized visor.

Crate 12 sat in the precise center of the reinforced flagstones. Its internal lead lattice was deliberately thick—a cage forged to smother the psychic radiation of whatever survived within. Thick cryo-rime frosted the edges of the steel, the violent thermal differential causing moisture to condense and pool around the base in a creeping white fog. The crate vibrated. Not with physical force, but with a dense, sub-audible psychic thrum that set the teeth aching and blurred vision at the edges.

A heavy lead-glass observation pane divided the command blister from the hot-zone. Up in the blister, the air was warm, smelling of brass polish and dry parchment. But physical distance meant nothing to the work at hand.

To slice a clean, intact skill-web from an Illithid-adjacent brain, physical proximity had to be absolute. The psychic bridge demanded a terminal anchor within three feet of the dying skull. Banki’s own physical frame carried too many conflicting arcane signatures; bringing his true body into the hot-zone would distort the delicate extraction geometry and shatter the prize.

Sable provided the vessel.

She stood opposite the crate from Mirell, clad in a matching hazard suit. Through the thick glass, Sable’s chest rose and fell in a slow, combat-steady cadence. She was ready. Operational consent had been established hours ago in the tactical briefing.

The incantation tasted of cold iron and stagnant water. The possession weave took hold.

The physical reality of the command blister dissolved, replaced by the violent, roaring transit of the astral tether. A microsecond of formless grey acceleration, followed by the brutal, bone-jarring impact into Sable’s nervous system.

The shock of entry rattled down her spine. Her pulse hammered against their shared awareness—ten beats faster than the resting rate left behind in the blister, a coiled spring of veteran adrenaline. Her muscle tissue thrummed with kinetic memory—the sharp, instinctual reflex of an operative auditing the room for threat vectors.

Sable offered zero fight. With terrifying military discipline, her consciousness folded itself back, stepping clear of the control levers and settling into the passenger seat of her own skull.

*<The floor is yours,>* her thought whispered—faint, relegated to the background, a ghost in the machine of her own flesh.

The hot-zone hit differently through Sable’s eyes and the polarized visor. The air was colder, biting through the thermal under-layers. The sterile, recycled oxygen from the rebreather tasted of copper wire and dry ice.

Banki pushed his psychic sweep straight through the crate’s physical seals. The invisible probe slid past the lead lattice and struck the brain inside.

The resonance bounced back—a jagged, terrifying echo.

It was no mindless beast. The scan did not merely map a biological mass; it mapped a predator that spent its life thinking about thinking. The consciousness generated a double-echo. It answered his scan, and a fraction of a millisecond later, it answered its own answer, analyzing the mechanics of his intrusion. A layered, sapient reflex. A fractal geometry of pure malice. It was Illithid-adjacent, and far too close to a peer conversation.

*Clean kill,* Null-Fang whispered in the dark of his soul. The artifact’s voice bypassed the possession tether entirely, thrumming straight into his core. Cold, precise, starving. *Loud truth. Zero second chances. The meat is rotting, but the secrets are fresh.*

Kestrel went rigid in the observation blister above. Through their secondary bond, her golden irises tracked the invisible currents of rising psychic tension. *<I hold overwatch,>* her thought brushed his mind—a heavy, steady anchor against the alien pressure. *<I will not interfere.>*

Through Sable’s hand, Banki gave Mirell a single, sharp gesture.

Mirell slammed her palm down on the actuator. The heavy solenoid clacked through the bay, followed by the violent, high-pressure hiss of aerosolized neurotoxin flooding the sealed crate.

Physical death required precisely six seconds. One standard combat cycle.

An agonizing, endless stretch in the psychic landscape.

Those six seconds belonged entirely to the skill-rip.

No crude, blunt-force trauma was used. That would reduce the neural data to useless static. Instead, an overwhelming, polite compulsion weaponized the bridge. A psychic architecture of absolute authority locked around the dying brain, commanding the consciousness to surrender its operational secrets with a structural logic the creature could not deny.

The alien mind realized its death at the exact moment its inner sanctum was breached. The double-echo collapsed into a telepathic scream that threatened to blow Sable’s ear-drums out from the inside.

Alien geometry and fragmented memories flooded the bridge. The structure of the creature's mind was non-euclidean—staircases of thought that folded back into themselves, entire libraries of stolen knowledge preserved in synaptic amber. Images of its hunts flashed across the tether—the cold, predatory logic of dropping onto a drow war-band, the bitter taste of terror as it unspooled the spinal cords of its prey, the suffocating, lightless pressure of the deep under-earth where it was birthed.

Its psychic defenses surged—a desperate, biological gamble against absolute subjugation. The entity attempted to construct an ontological paradox, flooding the receptors with the certainty that the extraction was not occurring, that the interrogator was merely a hallucination of a dying star. The sheer horror of the paradox clawed at his sanity, trying to rewrite reality from the inside out.

Banki increased the pressure. The statistical anomaly was crushed with cold, dictatorial force. The saving throw failed. The DC was insurmountable.

The operational baseline tore away. The lethal potential was stripped and cataloged—the synaptic pathways for cognitive splicing, the neural-lattice camouflage, the precise biometric triggers for mind-flaying. The data ripped out in pulsing, bloody chunks of raw thought, formatting the alien mechanics into ordered memory vaults.

Null-Fang circled the perimeter of the rip, devouring the psychic scraps, the terror, the agonizing realization of defeat.

The synapses collapsed. The mind shattered into ash. The double-echo flattened into a single, hollow tone, then plunged into absolute, permanent silence.

The threat ceased to exist.

The tether snapped back.

The violent recoil dragged his consciousness across the astral void in a fraction of a second. The physical re-entry was a car crash. The ambient temperature of the observation blister hit his true body like a hot wall. His heart, running much slower than Sable's, thudded sluggishly out of rhythm. The metallic taste of copper filled his mouth.

His heavy hand slammed against the observation glass, knuckles turning white. A hot, wet line of blood leaked from his left nostril, dripping onto the clean brass floor.

Down in the hot-zone, Sable dropped to one knee. She hit the flagstones hard, her gloved hands bracing against the frost-crusted steel of the crate. She shook her head violently, reclaiming control of her compromised nervous system, pulling deep, ragged gasps through her rebreather.

"Transferred and sanitized," Mirell’s voice rasped over the comm-link—a steady, grounded anchor in the ringing silence.

Banki wiped the blood with his sleeve. A heavy, metallic ache throbbed behind his eyes. "Log the resonance signature. Any future double-echoes are designated hostile on contact. Zero capture. Extreme prejudice."

## The Hierarchy of Stone

The facility demanded further order. The skill-rip provided raw data, but Sablehook required physical containment.

Down in the subterranean Registry Aux, a different problem waited.

The descent from the Pink Annex traded the sterile brass of the upper levels for the dark, reinforced spine of the complex. Polished stone walls swallowed all ambient noise, replacing the industrial hum of the air-cyclers with the crushing silence of the deep earth.

The door to the Controlled Pen stood at the end of an unlit corridor—a massive slab of black composite stone, thick enough to withstand a siege engine. Three independent verification matrices guarded the threshold, waiting like a silent tribunal that answered only to absolute truth. The platinum-weave sigils embedded in the archway alone had consumed three thousand gold pieces of refined residuum to etch—a security grid that guaranteed absolute isolation.

Stepping onto the threshold brought immediate physical resistance. The psionic pressure-field pushed back instantly—a heavy, invisible wall of force demanding a credential.

Banki threaded his resonance into the sigil-constellation. Not a push of brute force, but a perfectly machined key fitting a lock. He shaped his intent, projecting the core philosophy of his law: *Mine. Safe. Ordered.*

The divine-adjacent judgment weave audited the intent. It swept his soul for hesitation, for weakness, for the chaotic taint of the outside streets. Finding none, the system recognized the Authorized Anchor and stood down.

The heavy stone slab parted with the quiet sigh of expensive masonry.

The vault inside functioned as a fortified habitat. The air was cold, smelling of dry dust and ancient granite. A sheer wall of polished obsidian rose to the left, scored with deep, deliberate grip-slots leading to a high, shadowed ledge near the vaulted ceiling. Recessed light-fixtures cast minimal, angled illumination, turning the upper ledge into tactical high ground while leaving the floor exposed in deliberate vulnerability.

The Cliff-Skitter Predator hung halfway up the wall.

It did not pace. It did not crouch. It clung vertically to the granite—a nightmare of coiled muscle, chitinous blades, and prismatic, multi-faceted eyes. It was an assembly of intersecting razor-edges, designed to drop from the dark and sever a spinal column before the brain registered a shadow. Its carapace was composed of thousands of micro-laminar plates that vibrated at high frequency to shed kinetic impact. Its jagged outline distorted the air around it—a natural optical camouflage that bent light like heat rising off asphalt.

No greeting was offered. No submissive posture, no druidic attempt at kinship. The beast understood only one language: absolute control over the physical and mental space.

Banki projected his territorial authority into the room. He asserted the law of the vault, pushing his aura outward until it filled every corner. He set his shoulders, claiming the lower floor, making it explicitly clear that the flagstones belonged to the Anchor. Simultaneously, he ceded the high ground, dividing the room into precise, indisputable zones.

"That ledge is yours," Banki said aloud. The spoken words meant little to the beast, but the psychic projection beneath them carried the weight of an iron anchor. A shape of absolute intent—a blueprint of coexistence built on rigid, unyielding borders. "Perch there when you require distance. This vault is yours to hold. I am your anchor."

The beast stilled. Its multi-faceted eyes locked onto the floor, processing the psychic geometry. Those eyes did not merely read visible light; they mapped thermal signatures, arcane trails, and the microscopic electrical pulses of a beating heart.

The projection removed an uncertainty the predator was tired of carrying. The anxiety of unmapped territory vanished. A massive, scythe-like talon slid across the obsidian, the movement blurring the air into a shimmering streak. It shifted upward, dragging its heavy, armored bulk higher along the grip-slots, settling fully onto the recessed ledge.

It was not submissive. It was aligned. The predator recognized superior tactical positioning, accepted the boundary, and claimed its assigned zone.

The heavy stone door closed. The lock engaged with a deep, metallic thud, sealing the predator in its defined peace.

## The Tidepin Steps

The quiet of the lower corridor shattered against a spike of urgent telepathy.

*<Banki.>*

Sable’s mental voice was a razor-sharp needle driving straight through the hangover of the possession. Her mind was bruised from the skill-rip, but her operational clarity remained terrifyingly cold.

*<We caught a watcher. They breached the outer wards and slipped past Checkpoint One. They carried Darric's access token. They claim they were cleared for readiness mapping. The word used was ‘borrow.’>*

The ambient temperature of the hall seemed to drop five degrees.

*Borrow* implied an internal lane. It meant someone inside the network believed they possessed the authority to loan out access to Sablehook’s deepest vaults. Or, worse, an outsider was operating an insider who had drastically miscalculated the threshold for trespass.

*<Location?>* His mind snapped into cold, predatory focus.

*<Fleeing down the Tidepin Steps. Kestrel holds overwatch. I am dropping the lower bulkheads.>*

The Tidepin Steps. A brutal, spiraling staircase of sea-carved granite linking the deep vaults to the upper Mezzanine and the Reconciliation Room. The ancient access shaft was open to the coastal elements. The storm outside was at its crest; the Drowned Coast was famous for violent squalls that drove the sea upward against the cliffs.

The invisibility weave engaged.

The light-bending geometry slid over his skin like cold glass. The weave suppressed not just his visual form, but the sound of his boots on the flagstones, the rustle of his cloak, the very displacement of the air. A ghost slipping through the blast doors as they dropped, stepping out onto the precipice of the Tidepin Steps.

The roar of the subterranean tide hit him instantly. Freezing rain lashed through the iron grates, slicking the jagged stairs. Lightning flashed through the shaft, illuminating the spiral descent in blinding, strobing bursts of magnesium white. Every step was a risk—a wet, algae-encrusted ledge dropping away into the churning black abyss of the sea-cave below. Salt spray coated the granite in a layer of ice.

Frantic, disorganized boot-slaps echoed from below. The watcher was panicking, breathing in ragged gasps, slipping on the freezing stone.

Banki descended, tracking the intruder through the dark. The man was struggling with the brass-bound door of the Reconciliation Room, desperately jamming Darric’s forged token against the arcane reader. The magical friction of the false credential sputtered, spitting weak blue sparks into the gale.

Materializing from the invisibility weave directly behind the intruder required no blades. Steel was too intimate, too messy. Banki gathered his telekinetic authority, pulling the ambient momentum of the crashing waves and howling wind into a single, dense point of absolute pressure.

He drove the force forward.

The man's wrists and fingers were pinned flat against the freezing stone of the doorframe. The impact cracked against the granite like a broken spar.

No bone torque applied. No physical agony. Simply a new law of physics authored and enforced. The telekinetic field locked around the intruder’s joints. His physical resistance surged—a desperate biological effort against an overwhelming force. Banki increased the pressure, crushing the anomaly with absolute power. The DC was insurmountable.

*You may breathe,* the pressure dictated, *but you will not move.*

The intruder gasped, his lungs emptied by the impact. His fingers were splayed wide against the wet granite, completely locked in place.

"I'm not fighting!" the man choked out, his voice shaking above the ocean's roar. "I can't! I yield!"

Above, on the narrow, rain-slicked Mezzanine, the second watcher—the rear guard—tried to slip into the shadowed rafters.

Kestrel did not roar. She offered no warning. She simply ceased to be part of the ceiling and became an inevitable, violent outcome.

She dropped completely below the man’s perceptual threshold. To his nervous system, she did not exist until the moment of impact. Falling from the high beam-line, her massive bulk descended with the silence of a collapsing wall. She hit the flagstones directly behind his shoulder.

Her heavy forelimbs hooked inward with terrifying, calculated control. Her razor talons turned flat against his collarbones, refusing to tear the skin but promising instant dismemberment if he twitched. The grapple was won before his brain registered the threat. The opposed check was a mathematical certainty. She reclassified him instantly from an active infiltrator to secured property.

*<Target locked on the upper run,>* Kestrel transmitted, her mental voice completely steady despite the thrashing man in her grip. *<Holding for handoff. Perimeter secure.>*

The air in the Reconciliation Room smelled of wet wool, terror, and the ozone stench of the burnt token. The roar of the storm filled the silence as the telekinetic pin slowly erased the prisoner's resolve.

"Borrow." Banki's voice was flat, devoid of anger or curiosity. A cold fact laid on the table.

The man flinched, his cheek pressed hard against the frozen granite. "It was... a readiness trial! Darric said the outer lines were cycling! He said we had a ten-minute window to map the perimeter and pull back! Just an audit!"

"An audit requires an official ledger. It requires authorized sign-off. You are holding a forged token, standing at the threshold of a Level-Four containment vault, during a storm cycle. That is not an audit. That is an infiltration."

Banki pulled the token from the man’s locked fingers with a gesture. The brass disc was warm, thrumming with a forced arcane charge.

"Null-Fang," Banki sent inward.

*Let me taste the lie,* the blade rasped back, eager and vicious.

Banki pressed the flat of the token against his palm, opening a micro-channel to the artifact. No spell was cast; the sentient blade drank the residual psychic signature trapped in the metal. Null-Fang devoured the metadata in a fraction of a second, tearing through the deception.

*A cheap forgery,* Null-Fang reported, its mental tone dripping with aristocratic sneers. *Crafted three days ago in the Drowned Market. The signature belongs to a mid-tier artificer. Darric provided the base resonance, but the intent was purely mercenary. This rat was paid to map the vault, not audit it.*

The brass token dropped, clattering against the wet stone.

"You were paid to find the blind spots." Not a question. A statement of terminal fact.

The man closed his eyes, a thin whine escaping his throat. "Please... I just took the work. Five hundred gold to trace the ward-lines! That's all!"

"Who holds the contract?"

"I don't know! A proxy! A dead-drop in the Market! I swear on my eyes, I don't know the principal!"

The physical tells were absolute: the spiking pulse, the dilated pupils, the desperate pitch of his voice. He was telling the truth. A disposable asset—a blind probe thrown into the dark to see what bit back.

*Kill him,* Null-Fang urged—a slow, dark purr. *Drink his terror. Leave his corpse on the steps as a geopolitical statement.*

"No." Spoken aloud, addressing both the blade and the prisoner.

The telekinetic pin released. The sudden absence of pressure caused the man to collapse backward, hitting the wet flagstones in a gasping heap.

"You will return to your dead-drop," Banki said, staring down at him. "You will leave a message for your principal. Tell them Sablehook holds no blind spots. Tell them the wards do not cycle. And tell them that the next time they wish to borrow my shadows, the cost of the lease will be their head."

The messenger scrambled backward, his boots slipping on the frozen stone, before turning and sprinting back up the Tidepin Steps, completely abandoning his captured partner on the Mezzanine above.

A disposable mouth carrying a terrifying truth into the dark. The shadows in Sablehook were deepening, growing richer, heavier, and far more dangerous.

## Campfire in the Dark

The inner containment spine of the Black Labs thrummed with a vastly different energy than the upper levels. If the Pink Annex was a sterile hospital, the Black Labs were an abyss. The air was unnaturally heavy, saturated with the thick silence of a physical space that had been magically beaten into total submission.

The wardskins lining the masonry were not painted; they were tattooed directly into the granite using powdered cold-silver and basilisk blood. The containment runes thrummed with a slow, violet light, breathing in sync with the facility’s core.

Banki stretched his inner sight across the wardskins, mapping the boundaries of the absolute dark.

*About time,* Null-Fang sneered inside his skull. The artifact's presence slid between his ribs like a razor, applying deliberate pressure, demanding recognition. Null-Fang had been forged in an age when blood-magic was standard currency. It had drunk the marrow of forgotten kings and severed the tethers of minor gods. It understood no restraint. It knew only the hierarchy of violence, and until tonight, it had viewed its wielder as a convenient vessel to carry it to the next slaughter. *You keep summoning me like I’m a lantern. I am a mouth. I am a witness. I am a problem with manners. Ask me what I see. Don’t just swing me at the dark.*

"Null-Fang, stand fast." The acoustics of the Black wing seemed to lean in, the shadows pooling around his boots. "I require your assistance in this work. But we will establish the hierarchy of this room before we proceed."

Banki leveled a cold glare at the empty air, driving the full weight of his telepathic authority straight into the bond connecting his soul to the weapon. He forced the memory of the skill-rip into the blade—the raw horror of the Illithid-adjacent mind collapsing, the crushing weight of the ontological paradox. He fed a micro-dose of that terror into the artifact—a violent reminder of the apex predator holding the hilt.

"Kestrel is a sapient being," Banki’s mental voice vibrated with absolute law. "She is not an asset. She is not a tool. *She.* She is Family. We will grow together. You will afford her the exact respect you afford the hand that wields you. If you attempt to feed on her, if you attempt to belittle her resonance... I will seal you in a lead box at the bottom of the ocean and leave you to starve for a century. Do we have an understanding?"

For the first time since Banki had claimed the blade, Null-Fang did not posture. It threw up no wall of chaotic malice. It absorbed the dictatorial force of the threat, processed the unyielding boundary, and recalibrated.

*Acknowledged,* Null-Fang rasped. The sneering edge vanished, replaced by cold precision. *I name edges correctly, or I cut my own fingers. Kestrel. She. Family. Logged. The hierarchy holds.*

*<Heard. Understood,>* Kestrel echoed from her high perch above the blast door. Her disciplined focus brushed his mind—a warm, golden light against the cold steel of the blade. She didn't boast. She integrated the classification into her worldview.

With the blade brought to heel, the darkness of the Black Labs served as a private proving ground—a secure space to share their expanding power.

Banki produced a piece of alchemical chalk from his pouch. The scrape of chalk on polished stone was sharp in the silence. He drew three large, distinct circles on the floor, spaced ten feet apart.

He named them aloud, his voice echoing in the gloom: *"Family. Neutral. Enemy."*

He stepped into the center of the vault.

*<Watch. Observe the physics of the snare.>*

Reaching into his cold reservoir of arcane power, Banki did not merely cast a spell; he tore a localized rift in the material plane.

A writhing mass of black tentacles erupted from the flagstones, centered between the three circles. The spell—Evard's Black Tentacles—normally manifested as a mindless hazard that crushed friend and foe alike. But hours of brutal discipline had fused the raw magical formula with the structural control stolen from the Illithid-adjacent brain.

The shadow-limbs did not strike blindly. They obeyed the chalk.

Thick, void-born appendages, dripping with the cold of deep space, coiled gently around the *Family* circle, forming a protective wall. They ignored the *Neutral* circle entirely, lying inert on the stone. But above the *Enemy* mark, the tentacles lashed upward in a violent frenzy, crushing the empty air with force enough to shatter plate armor. The sound of the imploding vacuum was deafening.

*<I own the grip now. The spell is no longer a wild hazard. It is a targeted extraction. I can exclude your signature from the trigger geometry. You will never be caught in my snare. The field remains safe for you, even when I break the physics of the room.>*

Banki closed his fist, and the tentacles vanished, leaving only the scent of ozone and void.

Kestrel absorbed the tactical geometry. She saw magic not as wonder, but as an angle of attack—a variable to hold territory.

Banki raised his right hand, pointing two fingers at a lead containment block fifty feet across the vault. No vocal incantation. Pure focused intent firing an Eldritch Blast.

The violet energy snapped across the room, striking the lead block with a concussive shockwave that shoved the three-ton mass ten feet backward. The heavy lead scraped violently over the stone, stopping dead on a pre-drawn line.

The blast made no sound in transit. It left no visible trajectory in the air.

*<The blast is kinetic positioning. The Repelling invocation allows me to dictate the distance of an engagement. One clean hit shifts a line by ten feet. And because the energy is contained until impact, the vector does not point back to my position. I can strike from the invisibility weave, and the room will not know where the gun is hidden.>*

He tapped two fingers against his temple, his eyes burning with cold light.

*<Some minds are loud. Some are heavily shielded. But I can reach further now, catching intent earlier in their cognitive loop. And if they hide behind psionic walls, I can match the pressure to the specific thinker. No more unknown hands in the dark. Magic leaves tracks, Kestrel. Intent leaves tracks. I am learning to read the footprints faster than they can run.>*

Kestrel shifted on her perch, her golden irises tightening.

"Would you like to learn the spell to Fly?" Banki asked softly.

Her head tilted—a sharp, two-degree adjustment. Her crest feathers flattened in a rapid, involuntary slick against her skull. Her pupils dilated, her golden irises shrinking to thin burning rings around pools of black. One talon clamped onto the stone, scoring a fresh line in the granite. A single tremor ran through the flight-muscles of her folded wings—the predatory twitch of a body told the sky was available.

*<Yes.>* Then, a beat later, calculating the tactical value: *<Not for escape. For position. For joy. For better guarding.>*

*<How is it learned? And what are the rules?>*
