He stood, holstering Null-Fang against his forearm. It replaced the obsolete, primitive eldritch focus rod he had once used to shape concussive blasts. That rod was a tool for a warlock. Null-Fang was a weapon for a sovereign. Patient, thrumming with dark continuity, the blade waited for the inevitable application of its purpose.

## SECTION 3: THE RINSE BELOW

The Rinse Below did not exist on any official city map. It operated in the murky, lucrative space between high crime and high craft, buried deep in the sub-basement of a condemned Wharfside tenement.

The approach required relentless patience. Banki navigated the urban decay with the cold precision of a scalpel. The fog rolling off the docks reeked of rot, stagnant brine, and desperate poverty. He bypassed three false approaches engineered to funnel the unwary into kill-boxes. He descended two damp, crumbling stairwells where the stone walls wept black, foul moisture. He halted before a disguised doorway that appeared to be a collapsed load-bearing wall to the uninitiated.

The Crafters' Compass, glowing faintly in his palm, pointed directly into the stone.

He engaged his spell-cloak of invisibility. The magic washed over him, bending light and sound, erasing his physical presence from the material plane. He became a ghost carrying a void.

The breach was an exercise in absolute asymmetrical violence.

He stepped through the heavy door as it swung open to admit a shivering runner. The runner never registered his presence. The main guard—a hulking brute clad in patched chainmail, clutching a loaded heavy crossbow—stood guard in the center of the antechamber.

Banki drew no blades. He unleashed a concussive howl of raw arcane force.

The blast struck the guard’s skull like a warhammer hitting a cracked bell. The man’s eyes rolled back in his head. The roar of the forge from the inner chamber swallowed the clatter of his falling crossbow. The brute crumpled to the dirt.

The assistant, a wiry youth carrying a tray of volatile alchemical flux, spun around at the sudden displacement of air. Banki stepped inside his guard, tapping the precise nerve cluster at the base of his neck. The assistant collapsed silently, the tray caught by a telekinetic cushion before it could shatter on the stone.

Banki arranged the unconscious bodies neatly against the damp wall. He refused to inflict unnecessary collateral damage when minimal, precise effort achieved the objective. Wasteful slaughter was the signature of an amateur.

He stepped into the inner workshop.

It was a miniature, horrific cathedral of glass, brass, and stolen lives. The heat was suffocating. The air reeked of scorched ozone, boiling sand, and the sharp, coppery stench of blood magic. Precision instruments lined the benches. Vats of bubbling alchemical fluid cast sickly, dancing green shadows across the vaulted ceiling.

Standing with his back to the portal, the master craftsman—the Glasswright—worked.

For thirty-seven years, the elderly gnome had forged forbidden arcana in this subterranean hell. He manufactured amulets that projected false cosmetic auras to deceive city watchmen. He forged blades that boasted terrifying visual flares instead of genuine enchantments, sold to cowards desperate to look lethal. He crafted flawless identity tokens that permitted murderers to walk unhindered through highborn estates. His ethics were entirely nonexistent, but his craftsmanship was undeniable.

"Do not disturb the flux!" the Glasswright snapped, his voice a raspy, irritable bark. He refused to turn around, prioritizing his delicate, alchemical enchantments over basic situational awareness. "If the flux destabilizes, the entire batch goes up in blue flame, and I will carve the cost out of your skin!"

Banki dropped the invisibility spell.

The sudden shift in atmospheric pressure made the gnome freeze. He turned slowly. His gaze locked onto the dark, unyielding silhouette of the sovereign of Sablehook.

Banki bypassed interrogation. Interrogation granted the subject the opportunity to fabricate lies.

He reached directly into the Glasswright’s mind.

The telepathic invasion was absolute. Banki smashed through the gnome's surface defenses—the brittle shell of practiced lies and professional secrecy—and drove straight into his deep memory vaults. He ripped the skill-web from the gnome's brain. Names, client ledgers, black-market routes, blackmail records. He devoured thirty years of criminal craft in three agonizing seconds, each stolen memory searing itself into the raw psychic tissue behind his eyes like a red-hot iron pressed into flesh.

Two critical coordinates burned brightly in the stolen data: *Dry Kiln* and *Quiet Loft*. The next nodes in the hidden network.

Then, Banki tested a theory he had cultivated for months.

Normally, the Sovereign Thread allowed him to consume a skill-web and graft it into his own consciousness, leaving the victim a drooling, brain-dead husk. Tonight, he required a living vessel. He needed the Glasswright's specific, highly technical skills preserved for future, directed labor, isolated from the gnome's treacherous personality.

He located the instrument resting on a secondary workbench: a bone-fused gem artifact inscribed with microscopic marrow-runes. A Twin-Gem Reliquary. The dual-pointed crystal had absorbed decades of ambient High Magic in this forge, developing a resonant internal lattice capable of housing a mind.

Banki stepped forward. He pressed his left palm against the Glasswright's sweating temple. He seized the crystal with his right.

He reversed the flow of the spell.

Instead of drawing the mind into himself, he forced the gnome’s consciousness outward, driving it down his arm and into the reliquary.

The gnome’s mind shrieked. The psychic scream threatened to shatter the glass vials lining the shelves. Banki clamped a telekinetic vice around the Glasswright’s physical vocal cords, choking off the biological sound. The silence in the room became absolute, save for the spitting of the flux.

The crystal pulsed violently. Its facets blazed with the luminous, desperate exhale of a mineral lattice forcibly reorganizing itself around a trapped, screaming consciousness. One point of the gem glowed a warm, frantic amber; the opposite point shifted into a cold, clinical blue.

In the heart of the gem, a trapped awareness thrashed against unbreakable glass walls.

The Glasswright’s physical body slumped to the stone floor. His open eyes adopted the vacant, terrifying stare of an empty biological shell awaiting repurposed instructions. It was breathing meat. Nothing more.

Banki held the Twin-Gem Reliquary up to the sickly green light of the vats. The trapped consciousness pulsed frantically within. It preserved every memory, every spell formula, every intricate crafting technique the Glasswright possessed, perfectly ready for extraction and exploitation at Banki's pleasure. A wildly successful experiment. The Asmodean Pact in its purest, most efficient form.

He pocketed the reliquary, feeling the warm crystal thrumming against his thigh. He gathered the physical ledgers from the workbench, slipping them into his coat.

He looked down at the empty shell on the floor. On a clear space of the workbench, he dragged a piece of parchment forward, uncorked an inkwell, and wrote a crisp, exact command, leaving it weighted beneath a heavy iron crucible:

*NEW MANAGEMENT. REPORT TO SABLEHOOK FOR REASSIGNMENT. NONCOMPLIANCE IS INADVISABLE.*

Climbing back up the damp stairwells into the Wharfside fog, Banki did not look back. The Rinse Below was shattered. Its master lived in his pocket. He looked out toward the sprawling, dark silhouette of the city, fixing his focus on the remaining targets. Two more doors in the network still burned their lights.

He intended to extinguish them both before dawn.



# Chapter 11

## Talonscript

"Talonscript."

The dark, scaled tip of Kestrel's tail flicked once in the freezing air of the subterranean pen.

"Mothledge."

Her sleek, armored shoulders lowered by a fraction of a degree—so small a shift that only an observer consumed by total obsession would register it. Obsession was the bedrock upon which the core literacies of Sablehook were anchored.

"Family-only," Banki said, his voice quiet in the stone vault. "Public moniker?"

The response arrived faster this time, slicing through the chill atmosphere of the training pen through their mental link.

*Kestrel.*

A sharp, dangerous joke, perhaps, or a simple preference for a lighter knife when treading among ordinary company. Either way, Banki accepted the designation. Public and private truths were the two lungs by which sovereign institutions drew breath.

He reached into his coat and produced the crest-token—the dark, heavy piece cut specifically for house family seals rather than routine staff assignments. It was forged from unpolished ironwood and iron, worked with the house sign in a form subtle enough that a watcher had to look twice, and honest enough that the second look hurt more than the first. Grand, ostentatious symbols belonged to fools who believed affection required witnesses. He pressed the metal lightly against the scarred lattice along Kestrel's neck.

The reaction was instantaneous. The atmospheric pressure in the chamber dropped sharply. Recognition settled like cold dust upon stone.

"Good," Banki said.

Null-Fang, permitted at last to do more than stand rigid beside the threshold, sent a single, probing pulse of thought across the mental web.

*She is sharp.*

Banki locked his gaze with Kestrel's golden eyes. "She."

The correction struck the sentient blade like an iron finger flicked against a crystal goblet. Slight. Precise. Nonnegotiable.

*She is family,* Null-Fang amended after a brief, vibrating pause.

Better.

The harmonic overlay followed. Sablehook required no more sudden miracles that night, but certain doors, once unlocked, demand to be stepped through before cowardice tallies the cost. Banki crossed the dirt floor of the pen and laid his palm flat against the side of Kestrel's neck where the scar-lattice cooled beneath her hide. She did not flinch. Through her consciousness, the room expanded into vertical geometry first: beam, iron rail, wall stress, air velocity, kill angle. Through his, she tasted the dry, cold ugliness of administrative ownership—and nearly approved.

The primary bond anchored deep into blood, sinew, and marrow. The deeper tether unspooled between them—dangerous, volatile, and maintained entirely by mutual choice. A final depth remained untouched, waiting for a day when the world proved it deserved such a devastating union.

When the mental connection gradually eased back, both stood without embarrassment, making no pretense that the act had been ordinary.

He smiled then—a private, cold expression wholly unsuited for council chambers.

"Welcome home," he said.

Kestrel blinked once, slow and deliberate.

The nearest translation his mind could shape of the pulse surging back from her was this:

*Already was.*

## Audit of the Fourth Vault

The first true audit of the Fourth Vault occurred three nights after Kestrel's verdict.

It commenced with a bell possessing no clapper. The alert resonated through the bedrock instead: three sharp pulses through the stone beneath Banki's boots, a brief pause, then a single sustained vibration communicating to every cleared soul that the creature-side spine had awakened. The signal did not ascend into the upper compound; it did not reach the kitchens, the dormitories, the administrative offices, or any chamber where a timid clerk might confuse controlled violence for catastrophe. The Fourth Vault did not announce its existence to those unequipped to wield its secrets.

A senior night handler fell into step beside Banki as he cut through the torchlit corridor toward Direction Four. The report was delivered in rapid, crisp fragments. A disturbance during the night-cycle observation. No breach. No handler casualties. No structural collapse.

"Dire RhinoWhip Prototype One," the handler reported, his jaw tight. "Abnormal spatial testing. Repeated attention directed at a single containment transition seam. Altered tentacle deployment. Apparent anticipatory movement prior to handler correction."

The handler swallowed hard, his throat dry. "It executed a successful exploitation of chamber timing that should not yet have existed within its learned behavioral pattern."

Kestrel’s eyes narrowed to glowing slits beside Banki in the corridor dark.

"So it did not merely test," Banki murmured. "It solved something."

"Yes, my lord. The smart-room element—a timing-based transition gate, one of our newer containment features. Not fully breached, but predicted. The floor handlers felt the chamber had been audited rather than merely battered."

That was precisely the sentence that roused a sovereign in the small hours. Prototype One remained physically contained, exhibiting no signs of full frenzy. It was not battering blindly; it was already thinking in weather, ambush, yield, and spatial terrain.

Banki descended with Sable on his left—moving with her unassailable autonomy—Kestrel above and ahead in the ceiling shadows, Darric and Hesta Bramblewake waiting at the primary iron portcullis, and Kessa standing behind a reinforced observation slit with her hands folded as if attending a sermon conducted by jaws.

The beast-wards stirred around them, but only specific files commanded the room's focus. The First Vault handled routes. The Second held relics, residue, and dead things refusing to finish being problems. The Third wove doctrine into wearable armor. The Fourth smelled of hot breath and copper.

Tonight, the vault's active center narrowed to the creatures capable of altering a room the moment they entered it.

Choirless Siren Moths clung to copper hoops above the recovery corridor. A single hummed note from a captive hymnist made their pale wings unseal and devour the sound, leaving a silence soft as wool and just as suffocating. Protection, yes; also erasure, if deployed without mercy. Banki suspected Maren would despise them and perhaps understand them first.

Mercy Thorn Graziers lowered warm muzzles over injured handlers, drawing shock out through red buds along their crowns. Kessa had corrected him when he once termed it feeding: *It pulls the agony away. There is a profound difference.*

Mirror-Maw Canal Eels moved where the water reflected them wrong—a mouth appearing in the glass-still surface half a breath before the scaled body surfaced. *No free canal release,* Sable had decreed. Kessa nodded in agreement: *Net gates at every Crown waterline.*

The Stonemaw work remained mostly floor, tremor, brace, and argument. No living Gate-King breathed in Sablehook yet, but the floor learned impact, the braces learned weight, and the iron gates learned the difference between holding against collapse and holding a person in.

Kestrel's file stayed sealed. Banki had written the restraint in his own hand: *Kestrel is not livestock.*

The war-line came last because it had to. Teeth were simple to forge; purpose was difficult to instill. Musk. Blood-warm hay. Salt. A trace of ozone. Stone dust. Wet hide. The particular sourness rolling off creatures large enough to make terror the only rational response.

"Before we proceed further," Hesta said, her voice cutting through the heavy musk, "we review the doctrine."

"I know the doctrine."

"We review them because memory shapes itself around desire, and convenience erodes discipline."

Sable's mouth tightened into a hard line. Banki inclined his head. "Proceed."

Hesta held up a single, calloused finger. "No staff in a lane without two exits. No open gate without three wardens. No warden without a kill angle. No kill angle without a rescue route. No feeding during temper-shifts. No breeding permission until Darric signs body-stability and I sign handler-stability. No command test if the creature has not eaten, slept, and accepted a quiet hour."

"And if I overrule?"

"Then I resign in writing, loudly, with a copy delivered to Mara."

"A devastating weapon."

"The most dangerous kind: paperwork with witnesses."

Kessa's gaze remained fixed on the pen beyond the slit. "She speaks truly."

The pen held three prototypes. They possessed no official designation yet; Hesta's handlers called them RhinoWhips only when far enough away to be foolish. Dire Rhino mass carried an unsettling wrongness through shoulder, horn, and tentacle; the pen survived because pessimists had constructed the Fourth Vault.

Darric passed Banki the slate.

*Bones hold the new weight.*
*The shadow-slip takes root.*
*Lash-vines move with intent.*
*The void-blur falters under pressure.*
*Hunger eclipses command at close range.*
*Loyalty untested.*
*Keep caged.*

Banki read the final line twice.

The largest prototype turned its heavy, armored head toward him. The afterimage lagged behind the motion like a poorly rehearsed lie. For a single breath, the creature's true skull, its shadow-displaced skull, the horn-ghost, and the tentacle-blur all occupied different spaces. A handler behind the secondary gate swore softly and received no mockery for it.

Banki cast his thoughts outward, brushing the edges of the beast's mind. The Tri-Weave followed—Arcane, Divine, and Psionic threads braided into a single, lucid control architecture. Finally, he pushed the Sovereign Thread through it all, not to dominate blindly, but to test the prototypes against the law they were built to answer.

The room changed. Handlers ceased breathing. The shimmer around the pens sharpened.

Inside the reinforced holds, the prototypes answered—not verbally, but through heavy rhino mass, wrong-angle predatory drift, displacement pressure, muscle memory that didn't entirely belong to any natural beast, and the deep biological truth of entities forged to survive correction or perish trying.

The core body-plan correction held. The dire rhino chassis remained dominant and stable.

Kestrel dropped from the ceiling beam without a sound.

All three prototypes reacted. Recognition sharpened into active threat. The largest one's horn dipped; its tentacles spread with the terrifying precision of a killing engine discovering the room had acquired a target worth destroying.

Kestrel did not move. She merely breathed.

The moment arrived when the prototypes registered what most observers missed until it was too late: Kestrel lacked the shape of prey; she lacked the shape of a handler. She was an end-point—a creature that moved when motion proved useful and halted when stopping made the world come to her. The largest prototype held her stare for three heartbeats, four, five.

Then it lowered its head an inch—a clear acknowledgment of rank.

Darric exhaled through his nose. "Well. That answers one question and births six worse ones."

"It recognizes apex hierarchy," Banki said.

"It recognizes Kestrel," Hesta corrected. "Do not transform a single good sign into doctrine by breakfast."

"Noted." Banki turned his attention back to the slate. "We lock the breeding lines tonight. The override holds. Four breeding pairs. Each line-true, each distinct in spirit and intellect, all fully corrected."

Darric blinked, struggling to process the sudden leap from observation to full-scale infrastructure. "Four pairs? My lord, the gestation—"

"Three months," Banki dictated, rewriting biological reality with the casual certainty of a crown. "Working first-generation target of two-to-twenty-four young per female. Magical acceleration is authorized. Start drafting the cohort testing plans."

The researchers forgot to record the order. One of the specialists visibly re-evaluated the scale of the universe.

Sable peered through the observation slit at the three enormous bodies. "You want them."

"Yes."

"For trade caravans?"

"Eventually."

"For the streets?"

"If the city becomes a battlefield."

"For terror?"

Banki let the silence stretch a fraction too long. His hesitation answered for him.

Sable's voice dropped. "No spectacle releases. No proving-point deployments. No using them to make respectable citizens soil themselves because it amuses you."

"Respectable people so frequently require encouragement."

"Banki."

The name arrived quiet and sharp, cutting harder because it lacked the theatricality of a public rebuke. It was private even with five observers present—a dialogue between equals.

He studied the prototypes again. Teeth for Sablehook. Teeth for roads, kitchens, dormitories, children, workers, Blackwater citizens who had entered their names into his ledgers and believed, perhaps foolishly, that the Crown represented shelter. These would be guardians, if he could force the impossible shape of that word onto beasts constructed from siege mass and spatial misdirection.

"Crown Shield rules," he declared, acknowledging her authority. "If they ever leave the Fourth Vault, they protect routes, people, and wards. They interpose before they kill. They do not pursue into homes. They do not hunt civilians. They do not become a lesson I teach because my temper craves a blackboard."

Hesta stared at him. "Write that down," she instructed the empty space where Mara usually stood.

"I will inform her."

"No need," Sable said. "She will know by lunch."

The largest prototype breathed steam against the iron gate. Its afterimage breathed half a second late. Banki stepped closer to the observation line, careful to remain behind the safety mark Hesta had painted onto the floor in red salt.

"Keep them caged," he said.

Darric nodded grimly. "Without question."

"Yet the foundation holds."

"It holds."

The Fourth Vault possessed teeth. They were merely waiting for a mouth.

## The Heroes' Trail

Caelen discovered the primary dead drop on Tuesday morning.

The ranger worked the Dockside sector in a methodical grid, placing his trust in mud, loose cobblestones, and old tracking habits rather than quick cleverness. Banki monitored the hunt through the fragmented minds of the district—a beggar's dull stare, a stray dog's scent-memory, a gull's soaring loop above the harbor. The fractured surveillance annoyed him, yet it served its purpose flawlessly.

The dead drop was housed within a wax-sealed envelope jammed behind a loose granite block in the canal wall beside the Tidewash Steps. The location offered ideal operational utility—glaringly obvious to an operative who knew where to look, completely invisible to the casual observer. The parchment bore no wax crest, no identifying seal.

The enclosed intelligence was impeccable. The handwriting belonged to a ghost: precise, steady, and entirely stripped of personal nuance. Banki had paid an exorbitant price for that absolute absence.

Caelen delivered the envelope directly to Aldric within the hour.

From that moment, Banki reconstructed the heroes' preparations through a mosaic of intercepted perceptions. A street-sweeper catching sight of five figures huddled over a map; a dockhand observing Aldric's massive palm flattening a chart; a runaway child watching the elven ranger pry a loose floorboard and smile at the wrongness beneath.

Aldric refused to be rushed. That methodical, unyielding patience made Banki despise the paladin all the more.

Through the thin psychic tether bridging the district, Banki caught the crisp cadence of Aldric's orders.

"Caelen: verify the safe house locations. Physical reconnaissance exclusively—zero contact, zero engagement, no active scrying. I demand entry vectors, exit routes, and defensive ward signatures."

"Seraphine: commence preparation for restoration rites. If this intelligence is accurate, twelve citizens are currently living with stolen personalities, and we are going to restore them."

"Lyris: audit the song-stone network. I need to understand how the leylines link, which crystal serves as the anchor, and what occurs to the web when we shatter the root."

"Maren: locate the affected families. Quietly. Build trust. When we strike the Choir, these people must know someone is coming to rescue their stolen kin."

Maren spent the afternoon weaving through the shadowed doorways of the lower quarter. By nightfall, a silent, bitter grief radiated off her, bleeding across the psychic tether as she returned bearing six tragic stories and a single brass button torn from a missing clerk's coat.

She offered the button to Seraphine. The cleric brushed the tarnished metal, her consciousness recoiling. *The compulsion did not erase love,* Seraphine realized, the thought broadcasting so loudly Banki caught every vibration. *It built a stone wall around it.*

Maren held the button tightly in her palm long after she should have set it down. Cloth retained physical pressure; human minds did as well. Banki tasted her bitter wonder at what kind of entity could return a family member to their hearth yet leave the home colder for having saved them.

Caelen slid a clean piece of polishing cloth beside her elbow, acknowledging her grief without forcing her to speak it aloud.

Across the room, Seraphine whispered a prayer over the list of victims. The Dawnfather's golden light flared brightly, warming the parchment, yet the dark ink remained untouched—a stubborn stain of raw reality resisting divine erasure. Seraphine shivered, her unease rippling through the link, disturbed by the holy light's failure to cleanse the ink.

"We strike tomorrow night," Aldric announced. "The anchor stone first. We shatter the crystal lattice, sever the song-lines, and sweep the safe houses before the Choir can relocate their stock."

The party committed to preparation. Whetstones rasped against cold steel. Spells were memorized and bound into components. In the dark hours before dawn, while thick harbor fog rolled through the alleys, five companions knelt in a circle and begged their god for the strength to survive the coming slaughter.

Aldric verified every escape vector twice, then audited the side alleys other men had ignored. Mercy, the paladin knew, failed most frequently at the exits.

Six blocks away, Banki absorbed the sudden surge of divine energy—a warm, golden pressure slicing through the fog, conviction given the physical weight of weather.

The heroes would strike the Choir tomorrow night. Banki understood the precise architecture they were hunting. The Choir was no mere fanatical cult; it was an administrative behemoth concealed beneath the skin of religion. It maintained holding pens, illegal carry trails, and false ledgers embedded within the very bones of the lower trade tenements.

Something would inevitably go wrong. Something always did.

On Banki's worktable, his contingency map waited, its eastern corner pinned down by the bare steel of a knife.

## The Choir Burns

The captured lamp observer looked less like a hardened conspirator and more like a terrified clerk who had wandered far beyond the border of his own courage.

Banki discovered his observation post half a mile past Crooked Lantern Pool, where a grove of dead cedar leaned over a shale ridge, offering an unobstructed view of the road below. The man had constructed a small nest from oil-canvas, a long spyglass, a signal mirror, dry rations, and ciphered note slips sealed in red wax. A patient setup. Someone anticipated a long watch with minimal comfort; someone also expected to remain hidden.

Kestrel dropped from the tree canopy downwind without a sound.

She dragged the man backward through the thorny brush with one foreclaw hooked into his collar, her harness blade laid so flat against his jawline it appeared almost affectionate. Vesper followed carrying the mirror kit, her face set in an expression of quiet satisfaction at having proven a point. The observer's boots tore deep furrows in the mud. His frantic thoughts shrieked so violently Banki hardly needed to press his mind forward to catch them.

*Mirrorhouse sings. Count the supply wagons. Count the flesh jars. Map the iron chains. Map the beast lines. Sablehook is growing teeth. Do not let them take you alive.*

"A atrocious set of instructions," Banki said.

The man tried to spit; fear dried his mouth, yielding nothing.

They bound him under the lee of the shale, leaving Banki free to turn his attention back toward the city.

At midnight, Banki cast his mind across the district, anchoring his awareness in a rooftop feline, a wall-rat, two restless dockhands, and the ambient thoughts of a populace unaware they served as his surveillance network.

He sat far enough away to risk nothing, remaining close enough to study what Good executed when time ran out.

In the warehouse, Aldric's sword chimed against the first Choir guard's shield.

The shield shattered. The guard recognized the fatal opening too late.

Aldric turned the strike at the final instant. The flat of the blade, not the edge, delivered a blow with sufficient force to terminate the argument. The guard dropped into the canal runoff and did not rise.

Behind the paladin, Seraphine flooded the chamber with light.

The Choir's illusions dissolved. The warehouse stripped off its mundane disguise. Crates became cages; dusty tarps became operating screens. The space revealed itself as a fully functional holding-node—intake cages, sorting chains, transfer ledgers stacked against the far wall. Banki recognized it instantly: a second-tier custody pen, a false-ledger clearing house hidden beneath a common smuggling front.

Three clerks blinked in the glare, their borrowed, vacant smiles collapsing. Caelen's arrows pinned hands, bows, and alarm whistles to the wood. Lyris's ward swallowed the first hymn before it could burrow into anyone's skull.

Maren played her lute.

She played a steady, grounding line, and the altered clerks latched onto it. One of them looked up with clear eyes for the first time in weeks.

"Help me," the man rasped.

Maren kept her fingers moving. The song anchored the fractured minds by refusing to turn away from the ugliness of the room.

"Seraphine," Maren called out. "Three civilians. Still in there."

Seraphine rushed to them, both hands radiating warmth. One by one, stolen names returned. A clerk sobbed into his own sleeve. Another vomited black, oily fluid onto the floor and apologized profusely for the mess. The third kept asking if his wife knew where he was.

The upper level fell in four minutes.

Aldric found one operative still conscious near the trapdoor leading to the lower chambers.

The man had crawled behind a massive prayer-altar, clamping both hands over his ears as if refusing to listen to his own defeat might somehow delay it. He was young enough to harbor soft fear, yet old enough to have chosen this brutal work. Dark ink stained his fingers—Choir ink, routing ink, the ink belonging to a man who knew exactly where people were sent after their names ceased to matter.

Banki leaned forward into the psychic tether, tasting the man's surface thoughts. *Second Desk... Velis Rowe... Maelis... Harrow Fen...* The mental architecture of a vast administrative conspiracy spilled out in uncoordinated panic.

Caelen dragged him out into the open.

"Where are the altered kept?" Aldric asked.

The operative spat blood and clamped his jaw shut.

Seraphine stepped forward, a binding prayer rising to her lips, but Aldric raised one hand. He moved with terrifying calm.

"We have minutes," Aldric said.

He pressed the flat of his consecrated blade against the man's cheek.

Light flooded the room.

It carried no heat a kitchen would recognize. This was divine pressure—moral certainty made physical, blistering skin wherever it encountered corruption and driving pure agony through every lie the body tried to shelter. The operative unleashed a ragged, tearing scream.

"Where?" Aldric asked again.

"Below the cistern!" the man gasped, thrashing against Caelen's grip. "Left grate! Mercy! Please!"

Aldric kept the blade pressed to the blistering flesh.

"How many?"

"Twelve. Maybe more. I don't know."

"Who purchased the last shipment?"

The man sobbed—a wet, broken sound.

Seraphine's face drained of color. "Aldric."

"Twenty minds hang in the balance," Aldric said, his gaze locked on the weeping operative. "If his pain buys them back, then his pain is a cheap price."

Seraphine's light trembled once before she forced it steady. Her revulsion bled across the tether; the man on the floor was guilty, she reasoned, but that did not render the torture holy.

The names spilled out after that—not all of them, but enough.

When Aldric finally lifted the sword, the imprint of the Dawnfather's light remained seared onto the man's cheek—a second, permanent confession. Maren stared at the recovered map. A cold knot of understanding pulled tight in her chest: Aldric had saved lives, and he had done so by making a helpless man scream.

Banki, sitting in the dark miles away, went very still.

*So,* he thought. *There you are.*

Banki appreciated the revelation. Aldric was no fool constrained by naive pacifism; Aldric was a weapon that simply selected its own targets. The paladin had just shattered a delicate intelligence asset—a third-hand routing clerk, a man who knew where every body was moved and whose seal opened the next door in the chain—for the immediate gratification of recovering twelve missing people. Aldric cared nothing for Outer Carry doctrine or Dead Stone records; Aldric cared nothing that this man could have unraveled the entire ledger of Maelis and the Second Desk. Aldric saw only the suffering in front of him and broke the machine to halt it. It was a devastating, short-sighted goodness—the exact breed of goodness that left the root of the problem alive to regenerate tomorrow.

The descent to the song-stone chamber led through a trapdoor Caelen found in the north corner—the exact location Banki's anonymous intelligence package had specified.

Aldric descended first, his sword's light rendering the stairwell honest and ugly. This was the second throat—the hidden descent. Banki recognized the maintenance scars, the narrow stone, the smuggling passage built for moving bodies between sanctioned hands.

At the bottom lay the lower chamber.

Three crystals sat upon a raised stone dais, humming.

The vibration carried a low, continuous thrum. Lyris's protective ward absorbed the sound, nullifying the compulsion before it could breach their minds. The crystals possessed a sharp, geometric beauty. Through the tether, Aldric's cold pragmatism echoed; the paladin registered this beauty exactly the way a soldier registers the edge of an enemy's blade—as a feature of design rather than a redeeming quality.

"Lyris," Aldric said. "Analysis."

"Three stones," she replied. "The center leads. Shatter the anchor, and the song dies."

"Defenses?"

"We already bypassed them."

Aldric studied the crystals. He glanced at Seraphine, then down at his sword.

"Seraphine," he commanded. "Consecrate."

The cleric stepped onto the dais. She placed her palms flat against the stone. She offered no quick, functional prayer of battlefield healing; she began a deep, sustained invocation of divine authority—a declaration that this ground no longer belonged to the forces that had claimed it.

The consecration struck the song-stones like sunlight hitting black ice.

The crystals shrieked.

The anchor stone cracked down the center.

Silence rushed back into the room.

Aldric studied the ruined shards, then turned toward the prisoners blinking their way back to sanity.

"Secure the operatives," he said. "Document everything. Seraphine, begin the restoration sweeps. Every last one."

They moved to obey.

Later, in the Canal Row safe house, Banki allowed his awareness to linger just long enough to watch Maren attempt to tune her lute. She could not make the third string hold its pitch; the burned operative's ragged scream kept hiding beneath the note.

She loosened the tuning peg until the string went completely flat, then snapped the case shut without weaving their victory into a song.

Banki withdrew his mind, leaving the heroes to their poisoned triumph. He possessed the names now; he possessed the operational architecture. Tomorrow, he would claim the remainder of the quarter.

## The Dry Kiln

The Dry Kiln operated on the simple principle that the ideal hiding place for an illicit workshop was inside a legitimate one.

The building functioned as a genuine ceramics kiln: salt-glazed stoneware crafted on the ground floor, enchanted containment jars manufactured below, taxes paid on the legal wares and silence purchased for the rest. It served as a third-ring waystation—a dead-drop den for the broader smuggling apparatus Aldric had just blindly mangled.

Torren Ashpell managed both sides of the business. Middle-aged, stooped from decades in cramped cellars, permanently squinting from kiln heat, he was exhausted, underpaid, and desperate for someone to recognize the value of his craft without treating him as a replaceable cog.

Banki entered through the main showroom in human guise beneath the midday sun. His awareness expanded, catching Torren's surface thoughts as he walked past rows of pottery and paused to inspect a salt-glazed serving bowl that was, genuinely, exquisite.

"Did you craft this yourself?" Banki asked, turning the heavy bowl in his gloved hands.

Torren blinked. Customers did not, as a rule, inquire about the artisan; customers asked about the price. The fact that this patron had asked about the maker registered in Torren's surface thoughts as a novelty—a small, unexpected warmth in a daily routine composed entirely of transaction and tedium.

"I did," Torren said. "Twenty-seven hours in the salt kiln. Four coats. You can observe the crystal formation in the glaze if you hold it to the light."

Banki held it to the light.

"Preservation effect," Banki said quietly, tracing the crystal pattern with one finger. "It halts rot for a fortnight at room temperature. Possibly three weeks if the seal holds tight."

Torren's thoughts froze. The warmth vanished, replaced instantly by sharp, suffocating terror—the absolute panic of a man whose secret had just been spoken aloud by a stranger in his own shop.

"I am not here to threaten you," Banki said, before the fear could calcify into flight. "I am here to hire you."

He set the bowl down, reached into his coat, and produced a pouch containing one hundred platinum pieces—more coin than Torren's criminal employers had paid him in the last three years combined.

"This is a retainer," Banki said, binding the man with the irrefutable logic of an infernal contract. "It buys your exclusive services, your discretion, and your agreement to operate under my protection rather than the network's. You keep the kiln, the cellar workshop, your tools, your techniques, and your pride. Fewer people try to kill you. More people pay you what your work is worth."

Torren stared at the platinum. His thoughts raced through the rapid, desperate calculation of a man offered a lifeboat, trying frantically to determine whether the lifeboat was also a trap.

*...that's real platinum... a hundred pieces... I've never seen a hundred pieces in one place... but if I take it and the network finds out...*

Torren's fear receded by inches. Hope took its place, cautious and kiln-warm.

"The network," Banki added casually, reading the exact contour of Torren's hesitation, "is currently burning. The Choir is broken. The Outer Carry routes are compromised. The Second Desk will be dismantled before the end of the week. You are standing on the edge of a collapsing building, Torren. I am offering you the only solid ground left in the quarter."

Torren swallowed hard. He looked at the pouch, then at Banki's utterly calm, utterly certain face.

"When do I start?" Torren asked.

"You already have," Banki said. "Close the shop. We have a great deal of work to do."



# Chapter 12

## SECTION 1: THE TEMPORAL ENGINE

Time dilation does not merely alter the mechanical tick of a clock; it actively crushes the atmosphere. Inside the Breakfast Court Hall, the temporal shear thickens the air until every inhalation tastes of scorched ozone, wet copper, and the humming static of violent magic. One minute inside this heavily warded sanctuary equals exactly fifteen seconds in the sunlit world above. The temporal discrepancy transforms the grand chamber into a sovereign pressure engine. Commands go out. The dead return. Administrative networks collapse under the weight of brutal efficiency. Ledgers scream as their ink is forcibly rewritten by telepathic consensus. Yet the chamber holds its shape—beautiful, stratified, and entirely merciless, anchored solely by the immense, quiet weight of the throne.

Meat, heavily salted biscuit, thin bone broth, and clarified butter rest untouched upon the heavy oak table. They are the practical remains of rulership, the biological necessities that persist even when time itself bends violently over the anvil.

Sovereignty does not require puppeteering every knife-stroke from the chair. The Hall remains the beating heart; Sable acts as the hand. Out in the damp, blood-soaked field, moving through the shattered remnants of the blood chapel and the descent-sanctum, Sable operates entirely under delegated law—*Field Sovereignty Active.* The Hall awaits her return, synchronized with the slaughter unfolding in real-time beneath the city streets, feeding logic and commands directly into the combat zone without ever slowing Sable's blade.

The chamber stratifies into distinct layers of specialized purpose, each humming with organized intent beneath the Soft Epic Model B logistical framework, tracking exactly nine hundred and seventy-nine active followers. The ten-body discrepancy resolved itself hours ago through the accelerated integration of incoming ranks, seamlessly absorbed into the hierarchy.

On the center floor, the resurrected bent woman hangs suspended in mid-air within a ring of glowing binding runes. She no longer attempts to mask her identity or feign innocence; she is simply a local node in the enemy's network, dragged violently above the level of her own usefulness. Death provided no lasting sanctuary from the audit. The resurrection sickness leaves her skin translucent, glistening with cold sweat, her frame shivering violently. Her surface thoughts bleed raw, unfiltered terror into the ambient psychic field—fear of ancient names she refuses to consciously speak, exhaustion tearing at the frayed edges of her mind, and the absolute, soul-rending terror of realizing that dying did not mean she was finished answering questions.

Kestrel rests near the edge of the dais, her plumage bristling with aristocratic violence. The avian predator remains bright with the fresh victory of delivering the captive, watching the room with the intense, crackling pleasure of a hunting beast permitted indoors. She tracks every minute flinch of the suspended woman, a low, rattling click vibrating in her throat whenever the captive’s pulse spikes. Null-Fang rests close at hand; the sentient blade projects a deep, resonant silence that thrums against the mind like a purring predator constructed entirely of razor wire.

Vera Sorn steps forward, three heavy slate tablets tucked beneath one arm. Her face holds the unyielding severity of sharpened winter.

"My lord, the Hall is prepared," she announces, the words clipping sharply through the heavy, time-dilated air.

The interrogation resumes without a single spoken syllable.

The pressure inside the Hall shifts, focusing entirely upon the suspended woman. The Shadow-Tainted aura rolls outward in a suffocating wave, tempered only slightly by the ambient healing magic required to keep her shattered mind from fully collapsing under the immense strain. The psychic weight forces the answers to the surface, cracking open the mental lockboxes containing her deepest operational knowledge, ripping past her exhaustion and her limited knowledge ceiling.

She yields the logistical routes active during the systemic collapse—traffic centered around the lower receipt layers and the comparison handling chambers. Movement stuttered violently between the hearing-related spaces and the lower holding pens. Disrupted expectations ripped through the enemy hierarchy, sparking vicious internal strife over what should have moved upward and what should have been destroyed. The collapse affected multiple subterranean layers simultaneously, turning a synchronized machine into a panicked, self-cannibalizing mob.

Lower attendants stopped trusting their peers. Route runners deferred upward in blind panic, abandoning their posts. A sudden, paralyzing fixation gripped the mid-level handlers regarding authorized movement:

*Wait for comparison.*

The phrase repeats endlessly in her fractured mind—a frantic, echoing mantra bearing undeniable weight. It is the procedural terror that bound their society together.

*The mother-line one.*

Not a title, but a relational tag born of pure, biological panic. Panic over the blood-truth.

*Which line? Cleared by whom? Who authorized carry?*

The hidden chain was never simple human trafficking or mundane vice. It was a massive evaluative architecture—a sprawling, subterranean preparation engine for blood-marked, answer-bearing individuals. They sorted genetic traits and lineages, hunting for specific souls that could answer inward under immense pressure without shattering into useless fragments. Public house lineage meant nothing to them; they distrusted the declared house story and tested the blood-truth directly. The mother-blood frequently carried the truest door, watched meticulously over decades, marked in the blood chapel with living wall-pages and the bleached bones of failed ascendants. Children and family lines were monitored over years, named and revisited later when the awakening arrived.

Maelin and Halren work the sprawling map tables, translating the psychic bleeding into a city-wide anatomy of concealment. Bren Issel places heavy iron markers, mapping the false-routing and deliberate queue poisoning. The strategy forms rapidly: weaponize the deception, fracture the upper chain with contradictory orders before reopening upward, and maintain the impenetrable seal on the throat of the tunnels.

On the recovery side of the Hall, Mera Sol and Sivra Denn sit wrapped in thick wool blankets. They eat slowly, chewing salted biscuits with mechanical focus, clawing their way back to personhood. They stare across the room at the evidence lane, helping to identify the exact gears of the machine that tried to erase their existence. Eline Marr, Miren Vale, Neralis Venn, Ressa Vale, and Dara Pell huddle near the iron braziers, rescued from the chapel and restored to the living, their fragile minds carefully shielded from the ongoing interrogation.

The evidence lane stretches out like a morbid dissection table of the enemy's logic: a ruined leather glove, sheared cleanly across the knuckles; stacked slips of heavy vellum detailing maternal anchors and blood-wakes; heavy iron keys smelling of brackish water and ancient rust; a wax packet wrapped in silver wire, radiating the faint stench of copper and lake silt; tarnished pins used to trace lineages directly into living flesh; black registers, grouped slips, line tokens, mother-mark slips, reliquary caskets, and blood pages harvested from Morrow Glass and the blood chapel operations; and a gemstone containing the reconstructed memory of a line-comparison event.

Every single item represents a soul weighed, measured, and brutally discarded for the sake of the High Reckoning.

A directional silence drops over the ward-circle, severing the acoustic link so sound from inside cannot escape. The resurrected subjects are sorted with cold, medical precision: cooperative, panicked, catatonic, hostile, and medically distressed. Identity extraction begins—Person One through Seven. Each mind is peeled back, oriented, and catalogued against the master ledger. The Hall Return structure processes the living, the dead, and the anomalous remains with industrial indifference.

The focus shifts from the interrogation floor to the heavy, spell-locked isolation chamber in the deep background: the PINK Tier Lab, housing the living aberration.

The creature occupies a completely sealed arcane quarantine cell. It defies simple biological classification; it is not human, nor does it resemble any beast born under the sun. It is a mass of shifting, wet tissue—a localized biological contradiction cultivated within the dark confines of the blood chapel. Patches of pale human skin stretch painfully over rigid, asymmetrical chitinous bone structures. Clusters of mismatched, unblinking eyes—some human, some completely alien—weep a viscous gray fluid that sizzles faintly when it strikes the reinforced stone floor. The creature breathes in ragged, wet gasps, its flesh bubbling and constantly reshaping itself to match the fluctuating ambient magical frequencies of the containment cell.

It served the enemy as a nonhuman comparison subject—a doctrinal proof-organism, a heavily controlled anomaly in their twisted ascent taxonomy, used to calibrate the suffering of human captives.

Ordinary questioning remains entirely useless; the creature possesses no language, no structured thought, only a swirling storm of raw sensation and inherited agony.

Nonverbal first-contact initiates. The psionic connection bridges the gap, bypassing the need for a shared vocabulary. The mind of the aberration is a jagged, treacherous landscape composed of broken glass, screaming static, and blinding flashes of ancestral trauma.

The origin trace cross-references flawlessly against the blood chapel materials. The page-growth substrate—the terrifying living walls of the subterranean chapel that absorbed fresh blood and spat out the names of the chosen—echoes deeply in the creature's genetic memory. The aberration is a direct product of contradiction logic.

The enemy's core procedure relies entirely on forcing impossible paradoxes into the mind and body of the subject. The freeze-state formula reveals itself in the creature's physical trauma: *Anchor unresolved. Comparison spoiled. Do not carry.* The contradiction forces the biology to warp, creating a localized terror object that deliberately breaks the ascending chain, generating immense magical friction in the process. Contradiction-forcing remains one of the strongest procedural terror objects in the chain's twisted logic. Tokens, phrase strips, trays, packets, and bodies all share one single, bloody procedural grammar.

The upper layers of the enemy hierarchy fear contamination above all else; they escalate without descent, terrified of the ruined, broken things left rotting in the lower holding pens. The hearing-bodies—the unfortunate mortals physically modified to hear the deep chorus of the entity—are merely lower-chain instrument-bodies, discarded the instant they shatter under the sheer weight of the Listening.

The High Reckoning looms in the extracted data like a dark, crushing tide. It is an event—a harvest, the ultimate purpose of the viable souls and prepared bloodlines—hollowing out humanity to make room for something ancient and hungry.

Daelkyr authorship remains unproven. Null-Fang’s brutal assessment rings true in the telepathic link: this architecture lacks the alien perfection of the true Daelkyr. It feels entirely mortal—reeking of mortal arrogance, lesser beings constructing an ascent-engine from stolen, fragmented truths, butchering innocent lineages to build a door they do not truly deserve to open, playing with cosmic forces that view them as nothing more than kindling.

The aberration writhes in the containment cell—a grotesque victim of that mortal hubris. Its pain translates into raw telemetry. The page-growth substrate requires fresh human blood to function; the maternal anchors are relational and fear-topological, prioritizing the horror of the mother over the title of the house; the false-house contradiction triggers the automated purge.

The data integrates instantly into the active operational strategy. Updated sabotage templates form. Counter-ledger anchor-hypothesis rules lock into place. If the enemy relies entirely on strict procedural grammar to run their death-engine, that exact grammar can be lethally poisoned. A false token introduced into the blood chapel system will cause the comparison logic to devour itself, turning their own heavily warded architecture into a self-destruct mechanism.

Sel Veyran, Tobin Krail, Lysa Pellen, and two newly restored unknown humans wait in the stabilization wing, their identities verified against the purge lists. The children, the missing, the blurred-identity review—all city trace-back purges accelerate to recover remaining at-risk branches and hidden archive sites before the enemy can erase them. The auction settlement standard practice ensures all incoming consideration is itemized, scanned with psionic detection, appraised, and stamped, maintaining the release calendar without dropping a single coin. Mandatory disclosure forces the Sablehook Black Contingency Team to immediately report any cross-breeding or derivative work involving black or pink illithid-related items, locking the organization into absolute compliance.

## SECTION 2: PROJECT SECOND BITE

The logistics of the Hall demand constant attention, but the true crucible of the war effort lies deep within the beast-program: Project Second Bite—the Dire Displacer Rhino infrastructure.

The initiative shifted aggressively from doctrine-finalization into early execution preparation, moving with the absolute weight of sovereign construction priority. Rovan Pell, newly promoted to Head Foreman in Charge, oversees the massive overbuild package alongside Seris Vale’s provisional auxiliary core. Feed remains the primary bottleneck; Toman Hedd’s feed and protein structure barely manages to sustain the herd. A project of this biological magnitude requires staggering amounts of dense protein, and the supply lines groan under the constant demand of massive carnivores.

The true Stitchroom is no mere metaphor for the Hall above; it is a physical reality in the subterranean depths—a vast cavern dedicated entirely to flesh-weaving, forced evolution, and biological warfare. Black Lab Two operates as the primary testing ground.

The air in the Stitchroom hangs thick with the suffocating stench of copper, ozone, burnt hair, and raw, exposed meat. Heavy alchemical lamps cast a flickering green glare over damp stone walls. Immense, rust-pitted chains rattle against the vaulted ceiling, thick enough to anchor a warship. Reinforced iron cages line the perimeter, their bars scarred by brutal physical impacts and deep acidic burns from previous failures.

The visceral body-horror of the breeding program defies simple documentation.

Six massive Dire Rhinos form the foundation stock: the Caravan Line. Originally envisioned as the ultimate beasts of burden to secure trade routes, the project mutated into something far darker when the calculus of siege warfare hijacked the architectural blueprints.

They are no longer simple rhinos; they are forced hybrids.

The surgical process requires monstrous precision. The immense subjects are chained to the floor, restrained by heavy iron plates bolted into bedrock, and sedated with exotic paralytic venoms that freeze voluntary muscles while leaving the nervous system agonizingly awake and aware of every cut. The displacer-beast tissue, harvested at immense cost from the deep wilds, cannot simply be sewn onto hide like a patch; it must be woven directly into the marrow.

The grafting turns the Stitchroom into an industrial slaughterhouse. Teams of specialized healers stand by to forcefully regenerate torn flesh the instant foreign tissue takes root, trapping the beast in an endless cycle of mutilation and rapid healing. Acidic, glowing runes are burned into the rhinos' heavy musculature, creating permanent arcane anchor points for the displacer organs. Razor-tipped tentacles—the signature, deadly limbs of the displacer beast—are braided directly into the upper spinal column. The procedure forces the host's native nervous system to violently accept a completely alien spatial awareness.

Blood flows in dark rivers, washing over wide drainage grates. The beasts scream—a deep, chest-rattling bellow that shakes fine dust from the cavern ceiling and vibrates in the teeth of the handlers. The violent merging of two disparate evolutionary paths results in massive cellular rejection, localized necrosis, and sudden bone-growth that splinters through skin.

The first living proof of success stands trembling in the center pen: Proto-1.

Once a magnificent, reliable female Dire Rhino, she is now a towering engine of violence. Displacer tissue anchors along her muscled shoulders in thick plates of black, light-absorbing armor. Two massive, barbed tentacles rise above her forequarters, twitching with autonomous malice. When she draws a heavy breath, shadows around her warp and fracture. When she shifts her weight, the displacement illusion drags a fraction of a second behind—a shimmering, nausea-inducing afterimage that renders targeting her true center of mass impossible.

The tissue integration is a waking nightmare of biology. Her new spatial organs constantly feed her contradictory information: the displacer instinct urges her to view every shadow as an ambush, while her rhino heritage demands she lower her horn and charge the threat. The internal conflict manifests as pure rage. She snaps at phantom movements, her armored head swinging wildly, her tentacles lashing the stone floor and scoring deep gouges into masonry.

Darric clutches his wooden clipboard, his knuckles white. Hesta Bramblewake grips the heavy emergency kill-cord with terrified intensity, ready to drop the massive iron guillotine block suspended above the pen. Kessa monitors defensive wards from behind a pane of spell-hardened glass, her lips moving in silent prayer. Mirell, the kraken-kin scholar, watches from the aquatic tank connected to the laboratory, her massive, scarred tentacles shifting restlessly in the dark water.

Mental subjugation will solve the wrong problem. Mirell's internal notes remain clear on this point: dominating the beast's will through brute arcane force creates merely a temporary slave—a compressed spring waiting to snap back the moment the caster loses focus. Forcing obedience onto a creature experiencing constant spatial agony is a recipe for massacre.

The true solution lies in rank-cadence.

Psionic control applied not as a restrictive leash, but as a fundamental restructuring of the beast's inherent worldview—rewriting the definition of reality within the creature's mind.

Banki steps slowly toward the thick red salt line painted on the damp stone floor.

The working begins in silence. The Sovereign Thread weaves through the heavy air, glowing faintly with stolen heat. The magic does not seek to crush Proto-1's mind; it seeks the heavy, load-bearing tendons of her psyche. It bypasses surface rage, slides past the agonizing memory of surgical grafts, and taps directly into the deep-rooted herd memory encoded in her blood.

The psionic connection establishes a brutal hierarchy. Banki does not present himself as a human master; he presents himself as the absolute apex of the pack.

Concepts flow down the tether, translated into the beast's instinctual grammar:

*Hearth. Gate. Pack. Ward.*

Proto-1 lunges violently, slamming her bulk against the iron gate. The reinforced bars groan; stone walls shudder. Her newly grafted tentacles lash out blindly, seeking the unseen source of psychic pressure. The displacement illusion warps the air, hiding the trajectory of her strikes. One barbed limb hooks empty air; the other strikes the floor with enough force to shatter thick shock-absorbing tiles. Dust blooms in the air.

Kestrel’s mind slides into the psychic link, sharp as a winter blade. *Do not force calm. Establish rank.*

The rank-cadence pushes deeper into gray matter, organizing the chaotic static inside the rhino's skull. Displacer tissue is no longer perceived as a foreign parasite; it is reclassified as a tool for the survival of the pack. Spatial distortion is no longer a lie; it is a defensive shield.

*Probe. Brace the load. Catch the strike. Redirect the force.*

Proto-1 abruptly halts. White steam plumes from her flared nostrils. The shimmering afterimage stabilizes rapidly, locking into a controlled distortion. The frantic battering ceases. The beast stands ready, muscles coiled, blood dripping slowly from her altered shoulders, awaiting the pack-leader's explicit direction.

The child-shape test commences immediately.

Darric pulls the heavy lever. The winch releases. A child-sized heat dummy rolls smoothly into the outer testing lane.

The beast's predatory override engages instantly. The target is small, warm, vulnerable. The grafted displacer instinct screams for the easy kill. Massive tentacles snap open, barbed hooks spreading wide to crush the dummy into paste. Heavy muscles in her hind legs bunch, preparing for the devastating charge that will splatter the target across glass.

The Sovereign Thread pulses with an absolute command, overriding biology with hierarchical law:

*Warm-small does not equal prey. Ward.*

The tension in the observation deck becomes suffocating. The room holds its collective breath.

The tentacle slams flat against the stone floor, rigid as a forged iron bar, forming an impenetrable defensive barricade directly in front of the heat dummy. Proto-1 shifts her bulk with surprising grace, deliberately interposing her armored body between the child-shape and the rest of the room. She assumes a protective stance, her displacement field widening to completely conceal the vulnerable target from sight. Her head lowers, the massive horn pointed directly at the handlers behind the glass, entirely willing to gut anyone who approaches the protected shape.

Protector-grade cognitive hybrid.

The success is undeniable, heavy, and profound. But the cost remains exorbitant, the failures permanently scarring everyone involved in the breeding program.

Ash-Halter: the dark memory of the failed subject burns in the ledger. She was a patient beast selected specifically for the Caravan Line. The military graft took too deeply, mutating her personality. The displacer tissue violently rejected the gentle touch required to fit a harness. Her grafted tentacles barricaded the outer gate magnificently against intruders, but any attempt by a handler to secure a riding saddle resulted in violent, murderous spasms. The beast literally tore herself apart from the inside, her augmented nervous system screaming in excruciating static. The physical rejection caused the displacer tissue to attack the rhino host, the tentacles attempting to rip the beast's own throat out to halt the pain.

The horrific sound she made when merely attempting to stand still haunts the veteran handlers to this day.

The Stitchroom is a crucible of severe consequences. Success does not guarantee safety. The living components learn the concept of lethal pressure far faster than inert frames. A massive grapnel fired a fraction of a second early during a stress test, crushing a handler's hand into bloody pulp. Banki absorbed the exorbitant cost of healing mages, pouring his own vitality into the spell to reconstruct the ruined fingers perfectly, while the beast simply waited, executing its design without remorse. The beast did not understand the error because, structurally, there was no error. The flesh simply failed to withstand the mechanics.

Breeding is strictly prohibited until behavioral refinement is absolute.

The Dire Displacer Rhino project advances relentlessly into early execution preparation. The beasts will guard supply lines; they will shatter enemy formations. But they will do so entirely because the psionic rank-cadence forces them to view the kingdom as their personal herd, and fragile citizens as the warm-small targets they must fight to the death to protect.

## SECTION 3: THE EXTRACTION OF MEMORY

The absolute horror of the Stitchroom extends far beyond beasts; it applies equally to human enemies of the Hall.

Odran Vey sits strapped tightly to a rusted iron chair in the secondary processing chamber, a leather mouth-guard forced past his teeth. He sold names to the deep chorus for six months, justifying betrayals as simple survival. He provided intelligence that allowed the blood chapel to hunt the innocent; he pointed hunters toward mother-lines.

The interrogation does not rely on crude physical torture; the invasive magic possesses chilling surgical elegance. Liquid silver filaments slide down the psionic tether, bypassing mental shields entirely. The magic sinks into the motor-cortex, seeking mental knots where valuable secrets are stored: the complex lock-picking reflex, whispered countersigns, the instinctual flinch that signals a lie.

The extraction hollows him out completely—subtraction performed with razor-sharp psychic instruments, infinitely crueller than mere forgetting. Memories unravel rapidly from his tendons, rushing up glowing ethereal threads into Banki's waiting grasp. Odran suddenly forgets how to pick a canal lock; he completely forgets winding alleyway routes. He desperately tries to hold onto the memory of his mother's face, using it as a shield, but the silver thread bypasses sentiment entirely and rips away his ability to recognize human affection.

The physical memory of his betrayal is fully harvested. His right hand twitches against the leather strap, abruptly forgetting why fingers possess joints. The loss arrives as a shining line of subtracted skill. Each extraction hollows him further, leaving him present in the room but increasingly uninhabited—a vast house whose doors have been thrown wide, its furniture stolen, its empty rooms swept clean of dust.

Through the haze of violation, Odran stares at Banki's motionless face.

The complete lack of expression renders the agony infinitely worse. It is the detached focus of a craftsman shaping wood. Banki does not hate him; Banki simply requires the materials stored inside his skull.

When the glowing filaments finally withdraw, Odran remains breathing in the iron chair. His lungs expand with the memory of labor; his heart pounds a steady rhythm. His eyes track green light reflecting off stone ceiling tiles. He opens his jaw against the mouth-guard, entirely unable to remember why his throat feels raw from screaming.

Banki stands over the ruined man, flexing newly acquired knowledge, organizing stolen skills within his mind. A complex canal lock clicks open in his imagination, and he hums a Choir countersign flawlessly, using the exact cadence of Odran's stolen voice.

"Archive him," Banki orders the guards, turning away without a second glance.

The Hall remains the beating heart; the Stitchroom remains the unforgiving forge. The brutal war against the hidden chain, the deep Listening, and the looming High Reckoning will be fought with stitched flesh, stolen memories, and the crushing weight of sovereign time. Every horrific compromise made in the dark below serves to keep the sunlit world above completely ignorant of the true cost of survival. The Dire Rhinos will bleed. The captives will shatter. The ledgers will balance.

And Banki will continue to weave the Sovereign Thread through the madness, establishing rank in a world desperate for a master.



# Chapter 13

The Sovereign Thread anchored without resistance, plunging deep into the wet, thrashing meat of the beast and yielding to the raw, crushing weight of Banki’s command.

He dragged his consciousness through suffocating layers of living matter—dense, fibrous muscle slick with lymph; the reeking, bruised hide; the shifting, arcane roots of the creature’s displacement glands; and the wet, coiling tension of its muscular tentacles. Beneath it all throbbed the erratic, terrified cadence of a monster’s heart. He forced his mind past the scarred psychic tissue where the creature’s ancestral instincts had learned that handlers were merely violent storms to be endured. The corrected pattern held. It possessed none of the elegant symmetry found in quiet, theoretical spell-craft, yet it remained rigid enough to channel the magic’s brutal, tearing current.

Viable. Distinct. A female founder, pinned to the stone and primed for the working.

Sable leaned over his shoulder, her hot breath whispering across the cold shell of his ear. "Do not turn her into a machine."

"I am forging a bloodline."

She shifted her stance, the oiled leather of her brigandine groaning in the damp gloom. "Men only reach for the word 'bloodline' when they want a machine that bleeds on command."

He ignored the sting of her words and drove the magic deeper, twisting the etheric weave until his own marrow throbbed with backlash. The second founder could not be a hollow duplicate. The working demanded a violent restructuring of bone density, ligament stress, temperament, and the ingrained primal terror that dictated whether a beast viewed an order as an act of trust or the latch of a cage.

The Tri-Weave flared into blinding, sickly prominence. A thick, suffocating cable of High Magic, dense with the tyrannical obligations of the Asmodean Pact, struck downward into the subterranean mist. The air turned foul, thick with the stench of corroded copper, charred parchment, and dried blood. Law, marrow, pattern.

A violent, sickening shudder tore through Banki's skull. The subterranean air shattered like brittle glass, replaced instantly by the blinding, chemical glare of a Consortium operating theater. He was flat on his back, pinned to a rusted steel slab by heavy leather straps that bit into raw skin. The cold, mechanical horror of live vivisection ripped open his chest as faceless chirurgeons peeled his flesh back to the ribcage, shoving crude, heavy arcanotech directly into his pulsing organs. Cold Iron surgical clamps bit deep into his Fey flesh, hissing, sizzling, and rising in greasy black smoke as they seared foreign machinery into his living biology. He thrashed against the iron, choking on his own bile, fighting a frantic, hopeless war for sovereignty over his own body while they carved him into an instrument of war. The phantom agony of the Cold Iron lingered—a white-hot brand scorched permanently into his nerve endings.

He ripped his mind back to the dark subterranean vault, gasping for air. *Ownership of transferred infernal force is now fully vested in Banki.* The legal clause reverberated through the stone, an unyielding metaphysical truth hammered directly into biology. *Infernal origin preserved strictly as classification.*

He slammed the magic through the corrected founder template, wrenching a male counterpart out of the ether and into wet, physical reality. The magic extorted a brutal toll from his reserves, leaving the taste of wet ash and iron on his tongue, but the new male materialized with enough raw, territorial violence to immediately smash its heavy, bone-plated horn against the iron holding gate.

The female founder lowered her own horn, her guttural hiss echoing off the wet masonry. The male backed down, his neck muscles quivering as he yielded to a dominance he understood in his bones before pride could earn him a fractured skull.

Darric wiped a smear of grime from his chin and let out a rough, disbelieving laugh. "That is either the most impossibly expensive breeding pair on this continent, or the most extravagant way to duplicate a catastrophe."

"I assume it is precisely both," Hesta muttered, her fingers tightening around the edge of the observation glass.

Mara’s regulations arrived on a slab of heavy slate before the ink on the first breeding registry had even dried.

*Founder-class living assets. Zero unsanctioned breeding. Zero sale. Zero outside loan. Founder protection prioritized over production yield. Dual-handler minimum at all times. Mandatory stress discipline. Zero proving-point exposure.* She shoved the slate across the stone table, her jaw locked tight, fully aware that a single ambiguous phrase in her text could unleash a bloody slaughter.

"Three-month gestation," Mirell reported, her flat voice cutting through the damp chill. "Projected yield: two to twelve offspring per female, assuming the reproductive corrections do not rot."

Silence gripped the chamber. A single beast was a localized nightmare. A predictable breeding yield was political power.

"Four pairs," Banki ordered, his voice rasping over a throat raw from infernal backlash.

Darric squeezed his eyes shut and cursed under his breath.

Hesta spat a foul obscenity onto the wet stone floor.

Sable rested her gloved hand on the rim of the slate, standing beside Banki without a breath of hesitation, refusing the quiet safety of a subordinate. Her mind remained psionically linked to his, ready to tear into the beasts’ brains if the glass shattered. "Why push for four?"

"Because a single pair is an anomaly," Banki said, watching the beasts steam in the cold air. "Four pairs form a species."

"Species become history," she warned.

"Yes. They do."

Creating the four line-true breeding pairs required a grueling marathon of etheric surgery, violent behavioral refusals, and Kestrel’s unyielding, raptor-like telepathic stares. They survived Mirell’s cold, anatomical dissections and Mara’s suffocating legal chains. By the time Direction Four held four stable founder pairs in its deep pens, a heavy, bone-deep exhaustion lay over the underground complex.

Banki exhaled slowly, letting the residual spellfire settle into a dull, throb behind his eyes, fully conscious of the razor-thin margin between a historic triumph and a catastrophic mistake.

## The Branch Strains

The specialized strains split naturally from the raw biological stock.

Branch A claimed the heavy road-guardians. The Caravan Line learned to carry staggering tonnage long before they were introduced to fear. A massive pair, Black-Yoke and Lantern-Rib, emerged as the undisputed anchors, kneeling placidly in the mud for harness fittings and rising without a tremor beneath massive loads of iron and stone. They kept their thick, coiled tentacles tucked neatly away from the yoke-lines even when handlers popped hidden ambush banners directly in their faces. They dragged weighted iron sleds through hair-pin turns, screaming crowds, and staged wheel-failures without spilling a single drop of blood or missing a stride.

During one drill, Black-Yoke braced a sliding, ten-ton supply wagon with a single, muscular tentacle, his hooves plowing deep furrows into the dirt. Lantern-Rib reached down and lifted a fallen, screaming handler by the collar of his oilskin coat, placing the man gently behind the timber safety rail with the terrifying, effortless delicate precision of a siege engine pouring wine.

Darric dragged his chalk across the slate with a sharp, approving stroke. Hesta snatched the slate from his hands, scrawling a dark warning beneath his signature regarding the profound gulf between physical obedience and actual civilization.

Branch C cultivated the discipline. The Refinement Line subjected older beasts to unpredictable handlers, deafening brass bells, simulated pain, and prolonged starvation. Soft-Chain Mother emerged as the matriarch of the line—a massive, scarred creature capable of checking three thrashing juveniles at a threshold simply by shifting her immense weight and striking the stone floor once with the hardened tip of a tentacle.

"That one," Kestrel’s voice projected into their minds, sharp as a striking beak.

The handlers froze, silent. The avian predator rarely deigned to use spoken words when telepathy was available.

"She teaches," Kestrel added, her golden eyes fixed on the beast.

Branch D kept the horrors. Everything that emerged wrong from the womb—twisted spines, ruptured venom glands, recursive pain loops, and broken command lattices—was funneled down into the dark holding vaults. Hesta insisted on giving every condemned creature a name before the surgical trays were unsealed. After the third name, Darric stopped trying to break the silence with jokes.

Mara never set foot in Branch D a second time. She simply went back to her desk and drafted tighter laws.

## The Military Line

Banki had originally envisioned the Caravan Line—great, docile beasts coated in road-dust, hauling medicine and grain through bandit-infested territories. But the blood and corruption of Blackwater Quay had forced a darker arithmetic upon him. Venal guards and predatory cartels did not yield to grain wagons. They yielded to fangs.

He recalled Black-Yoke kneeling in the mud while a rescued child pressed her tiny hand against his armored brow. A distant dock-bell had clanged, the child had flinched, and Black-Yoke had instantly swung his massive, horned head toward the noise with devastating speed. The beast possessed no doctrine for punishing the thing that caused a child terror. It only knew how to kill.

Sable leaned against the upper gantry rail, watching the shadows warp around his face. "You are about to transform a dream into an engine of slaughter."

Above them, Kestrel tilted her feathered head, her razor-sharp talons biting deep into the wooden beam. Her apex-predator instincts rippled down their psychic bond: a caravan beast could protect a road, but a war-beast could make the road too terrifying for cages to exist.

Fourteen heavy war-forms paced behind the reinforced glass of the low tier. Captain Maela Thorn-Rusk surveyed the monsters with the cold, unblinking assessment of a veteran mercenary. "You have the seed of a war-beast corps here. Field them a day too soon, and you will produce a massacre that merely happens to consume your enemies first."

"I am aware."

"You are informed," she corrected crisply, her gauntleted finger tapping the heavy glass. "Awareness only strikes you after you watch a beast cross a boundary you swore it understood."

Banki summoned the Tri-Weave once more. The magic answered differently to the Military Strain, rising thick with the stench of scorched gates, shattered shields, and unburied dead. The infernal conduit remained strictly optional, an isolated channel activated only by express, recorded invocation under the strict parameters of their agreement. He drew the infernal power into the room, accepting the crushing burden frame without a flicker of hesitation.

Kestrel’s mind locked into his, untroubled by the colossal physical mass of the creatures below. Beside him, Null-Fang hovered at his shoulder, the spectral weapon's haft-mouth slavering as it tasted the sudden drop in atmospheric pressure.

*Bite the weak commands first,* Null-Fang hissed into the dark of his mind.

Kestrel slashed through the spectral blade's thought instantly. *Make their minds impossible to break.*

The Sovereign Thread hardened into an unyielding cable of Pact-magic. Banki hammered the raw power into all fourteen war-beasts, testing their survival instincts against his mandated behavioral constraints. He drove the spellcraft to a terrifying crest, deliberately shattering the underlying arcane structure. He refused to clone them. Cloning brought genetic stagnation and fragile minds. Instead, he forced a violent biological triplication, fracturing the fourteen original bodies into forty-two distinct, living entities—each bearing subtle variations in armor plating and neurological routing to guarantee survival.
